## Source: `07_Evidence_and_Reading/Landmark_Library/Primary_Source_Download_List.md`

# Primary Source Download List - Adult Inpatient Psychiatry Clerkship

**For:** Joshua Moss, MD * **Use:** download the actual PDFs via MaineHealth institutional access and file them beside the Landmark library so trainees read primary sources, not summaries.
**Citations** are drawn from your Landmark Teaching Guide + standard instrument sources. Lookup key = **first author * year * journal**; titles are given so you can paste straight into PubMed or the journal. *Verify volume/page before any formal citation.*

> **How to pull efficiently at MaineHealth**
> 1. **PubMed** -> search the title -> click the journal/**"Find It @ MaineHealth"** (LinkResolver) for full text.
> 2. **LibKey / LibKey Nomad** (browser extension) gives one-click PDF on PubMed/Google Scholar through your institution - fastest for a batch like this.
> 3. Direct publisher access is covered for **NEJM, The Lancet (+ Lancet Psychiatry), JAMA/JAMA Psychiatry, AJP, Br J Psychiatry, Schizophrenia Bulletin, Cochrane** via the MaineHealth Library proxy.
> 4. For anything paywalled with no full text, the **MaineHealth Library document-delivery / ILL** service will retrieve it.
> Access flags below:  usually open-access *  paywalled but covered by your access *  book (library/ILL).

---

## TIER 1 - The rotation core (download all 16 first)
These are the 15/16-paper reading pathway. Every trainee reads these; they anchor the 6-week curriculum and the 6 Journal-Club packets.

| # | Citation (author * year * journal) | Title | Why | Access |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Engel * 1977 * *Science* 196:129 | The need for a new medical model: a challenge for biomedicine | The biopsychosocial operating system |  |
| 2 | Rosenhan * 1973 * *Science* 179:250 | On being sane in insane places | Diagnostic humility / labeling |  |
| 3 | Appelbaum & Grisso * 1988 * *NEJM* 319:1635 | Assessing patients' capacities to consent to treatment | The four-abilities capacity model |  |
| 4 | Stanley & Brown * 2012 * *Cogn Behav Pract* 19:256 | Safety Planning Intervention | The most practical tool on the list |  |
| 5 | Lieberman et al. (CATIE) * 2005 * *NEJM* 353:1209 | Effectiveness of antipsychotic drugs in chronic schizophrenia | Pick by profile, not class |  |
| 6 | Rush et al. (STAR*D) * 2006 * *AJP* 163:1905 | Acute & longer-term outcomes in depressed outpatients (steps) | Measurement-based care |  |
| 7 | Brown, Birley & Wing * 1972 * *Br J Psychiatry* 121:241 | Influence of family life on the course of schizophrenic disorders | Expressed emotion -> relapse |  |
| 8 | Bush et al. * 1996 * *Acta Psychiatr Scand* 93:129 | Catatonia I: rating scale and standardized examination | BFCRS; the dx you can't miss |  |
| 9 | Wampold et al. * 1997 * *Psychol Bull* 122:203 | Bona fide psychotherapies: "all must have prizes" | Common factors |  |
| 10 | Linehan et al. * 1991 * *Arch Gen Psychiatry* 48:1060 | CBT of chronically parasuicidal borderline patients | DBT origin; BPD is treatable |  |
| 11 | Pharoah et al. * 2010 * *Cochrane* CD000088 | Family intervention for schizophrenia | NNT ~7 for relapse |  |
| 12 | March et al. (TADS) * 2004 * *JAMA* 292:807 | Fluoxetine, CBT, and their combination for adolescent depression | Combination best for teens |  |
| 13 | Felitti et al. (ACE) * 1998 * *Am J Prev Med* 14:245 | Childhood abuse/household dysfunction & leading causes of death | Reframes every patient |  |
| 14a | Caspi et al. * 2003 * *Science* 301:386 | 5-HTTLPR  life stress moderation of depression | Candidate-gene rise... |  |
| 14b | Border et al. * 2019 * *AJP* 176:376 | No support for historical candidate-gene hypotheses for depression | ...and fall (replication crisis) |  |
| 15 | Franklin et al. * 2017 * *Psychol Bull* 143:187 | Risk factors for suicidal thoughts/behaviors: 50-year meta-analysis | We can't predict; document reasoning |  |
| 16 | Volkow, Koob & McLellan * 2016 * *NEJM* 374:363 | Neurobiologic advances from the brain disease model of addiction | Combats stigma |  |

---

## TIER 2 - Topic deepening (download by interest / teaching need)

### Psychopharmacology - modern landmark trials (great use of your Lancet/NEJM/JAMA access)
- Cipriani et al. * 2018 * *Lancet* 391:1357 - Comparative efficacy/acceptability of 21 antidepressants (network meta-analysis). 
- Huhn et al. * 2019 * *Lancet* 394:939 - Comparative efficacy of 32 antipsychotics (network meta-analysis). 
- Leucht et al. * 2013 * *Lancet* 382:951 - Antipsychotics vs placebo, multiple-treatments meta-analysis. 
- Cipriani et al. * 2013 * *BMJ* 346:f3646 - Lithium in mood disorders and suicide prevention (meta-analysis). 
- BALANCE investigators * 2010 * *Lancet* 375:385 - Lithium  valproate for relapse prevention in bipolar I. 
- Tiihonen et al. * 2009 * *Lancet* 374:620 - Real-world antipsychotics and mortality in schizophrenia. 

### Family & systems (your signature area)
- McFarlane * 1997 * *Clin Handbook of Couple Therapy* (ch.) - Psychoeducational multifamily groups. 
- Miklowitz & Goldstein * 1997 * Guilford (book) - Bipolar Disorder: A Family-Focused Treatment Approach. 
- Falloon * 1992 * *Family Therapy and Mental Health* (ch.) - Behavioral family therapy. 

### Suicide & safety (pairs with your tools)
- Posner et al. * 2011 * *AJP* 168:1266 - The Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS). 
- Stanley et al. * 2018 * *JAMA Psychiatry* 75:894 - Safety Planning Intervention RCT (45% fewer attempts in the ED). 
- Jobes * 2012 * *Suicide Life Threat Behav* 42:640 - CAMS: collaborative assessment & management of suicidality. 

### Catatonia
- Fink & Taylor * 2003 * Cambridge (book) - Catatonia: A Clinician's Guide to Diagnosis and Treatment. 
- Walther & Strik * 2012 * *Neuropsychobiology* 66:57 - Psychomotor symptoms map onto the motor system. 

### Capacity, ethics & culture
- Grisso & Appelbaum * 1995 * *Law Hum Behav* / MacArthur Competence studies - the empirical basis of the model. 
- Lewis-Fernandez et al. * 2016 * *Psychiatr Serv* 67:192 - The DSM-5 Cultural Formulation Interview. 
- Kleinman * 1978 * *Soc Sci Med* 12:85 - Explanatory models of illness. 

### Substance use & withdrawal (pairs with your CIWA/COWS card)
- Sullivan et al. * 1989 * *Br J Addict* 84:1353 - CIWA-Ar validation. 
- Wesson & Ling * 2003 * *J Psychoactive Drugs* 35:253 - Clinical Opiate Withdrawal Scale (COWS). 
- Mattick et al. * 2014 * *Cochrane* CD002207 - Buprenorphine vs methadone maintenance. 

### Violence / agitation (pairs with your Violence one-pager)
- Almvik, Woods & Rasmussen * 2000 * *J Interpers Violence* 15:1284 - Brset Violence Checklist validation. 
- Wilson et al. (Project BETA) * 2012 * *West J Emerg Med* 13:26 - Psychopharmacology of agitation consensus. 
- Richmond et al. (Project BETA) * 2012 * *West J Emerg Med* 13:17 - Verbal de-escalation of the agitated patient. 

### Early psychosis
- McGorry, Killackey & Yung * 2008 * *World Psychiatry* 7:148 - Early intervention in psychosis. 
- Kane et al. (RAISE) * 2016 * *AJP* 173:362 - Coordinated specialty care for first-episode psychosis. 

### Mood, sleep & circadian
- Harvey et al. * 2015 * *J Affect Disord* 184:41 - Treating sleep disturbance improves mood in bipolar. 
- Frank et al. * 2005 * *Arch Gen Psychiatry* 62:996 - Interpersonal & Social Rhythm Therapy (IPSRT) outcomes. 

### Recovery, adherence & meaning
- Velligan et al. * 2009 * *J Clin Psychiatry* 70(suppl 4):1 - Adherence problems in serious mental illness (expert consensus). 
- Deegan * 1996 * *Psychiatr Rehabil J* 19:91 - Recovery as a journey of the heart. 

### Neurodiversity, geriatric & differential
- Lai & Baron-Cohen * 2015 * *Lancet Psychiatry* 2:1013 - Identifying the lost diagnosis: autism in adults. 
- Caine * 1981 * *Arch Gen Psychiatry* 38:1359 - Pseudodementia (depression vs neurodegeneration). 
- Alexopoulos et al. * 1997 * *Arch Gen Psychiatry* 54:915 - The vascular depression hypothesis. 

---

## TIER 3 - Screening & rating instruments (the validation papers)
Download these so trainees use the tools correctly and can cite them. (Several are open or freely distributed by the authors.)
- Kroenke, Spitzer & Williams * 2001 * *J Gen Intern Med* 16:606 - **PHQ-9**. 
- Spitzer et al. * 2006 * *Arch Intern Med* 166:1092 - **GAD-7**. 
- Hirschfeld et al. * 2000 * *AJP* 157:1873 - **Mood Disorder Questionnaire (MDQ)**. 
- Bush et al. * 1996 (above) - **Bush-Francis Catatonia Rating Scale**. 
- Posner et al. * 2011 (above) - **C-SSRS**. 
- Sullivan 1989 / Wesson 2003 / Almvik 2000 (above) - **CIWA-Ar / COWS / Brset**.
- Bush, Fink, Petrides * 1996 (II) * *Acta Psychiatr Scand* 93:137 - catatonia treatment/lorazepam response (companion to #8). 

---

## TIER 4 - Guidelines & society documents (often free; not journal "primary sources")
- **APA Practice Guideline for the Treatment of Patients With Schizophrenia**, 3rd ed., 2021.  (psychiatryonline)
- **APA Practice Guideline - Major Depressive Disorder** (and the forthcoming/updated MDD guideline). 
- **APA Practice Guideline - Bipolar Disorder**; **Assessment & Treatment of Patients With Suicidal Behaviors**. 
- **The Joint Commission** NPSG suicide-risk (sentinel-event alert 56). 
- **ASAM** alcohol withdrawal management guideline (2020). 
- **NICE** guidance: violence and aggression (NG10), self-harm (NG225), bipolar (CG185). 
> Your existing `~/Inpatient_Psychiatry_Guideline_Surveillance_2023-2026.docx` already tracks the current versions - pull from there for the latest.

---

## Suggested download workflow
1. **Batch Tier 1** (16) into a `Landmark_Library/PDFs/` folder named `## - FirstAuthor - Year.pdf` (matches your existing Psychodynamic reading-list convention).
2. Add **Tier 3 instruments** (used daily on the unit).
3. Pull **Tier 2** by the week you're teaching (e.g., Week 4 -> the family/EE set).
4. Treat **Tier 4** as living links, not downloads - they version.

## Companion checklist (verified IDs + tracker)
`Primary_Source_Download_Checklist.xlsx` (same folder) has every item as a filterable row with **verified PMIDs + DOIs** (sourced from PubMed/NLM), clickable `doi.org` links, an access flag, and a **Downloaded [yes]** column with an auto-updating progress tab. 32 of 37 journal articles resolved to a PMID/DOI; the 5 without are journals PubMed doesn't index (Cognitive & Behavioral Practice, J Interpersonal Violence, Psychiatr Rehabil J, Psychological Bulletin pre-coverage) - find those by title.


---

## Source: `07_Evidence_and_Reading/Landmark_Library/_source/2026-03-31-griefspace-quick-actions-interactivity-design.md`

# GriefSpace Quick Actions Interactivity

**Date:** 2026-03-31
**Status:** Approved
**Scope:** `tools-suite/tools/generated/griefspace-app.app.jsx`, `tools-suite/tools/griefspace-app.html`

## Summary

Add interactivity, completion tracking, inline timers, cross-tool links, Patterns integration, and expanded content to the existing Quick Actions (MICRO_ACTIONS) system in GriefSpace.

## Current State

- `MICRO_ACTIONS`: static object mapping 8 emotions -> 3 actions each (24 total)
- Each action: `{ id, emoji, title, desc, type }` where type  {grounding, breath, body, expression}
- `MicroActionPanel` component renders inside `ModuleScreen` (pathway lesson pages)
- Already has: `logAction()` writing to `STORAGE.MICRO_LOG`, inline breathing timer for `type: 'breath'`, expand/collapse detail per action type
- `MICRO_LOG` schema: `{ id, emotion, action, timestamp }`
- Dashboard (`DashboardScreen`) does not reference MICRO_LOG

## Changes

### 1. "I tried this" button + warm confirmation

**Location:** Bottom of each expanded Quick Action detail panel in `MicroActionPanel`.

**Behavior:**
1. Button label: **"I tried this [yes]"** (subtle outline style, not primary)
2. On tap: button text swaps to **"Good. That matters."** with 300ms fade-in animation, button becomes non-interactive
3. After 2.5 seconds, text transitions to a persistent muted **"[yes] Done"** state (remains for the rest of the session)
4. Calls `logAction(action)` with `completed: true` flag (see data changes below)
5. On the Quick Action card itself (collapsed state), show a small `[yes]` badge in the top-right corner for any action the user has previously completed
6. Badge check reads from MICRO_LOG: if any entry has `action === act.id && completed === true`, show badge

**State:** `completedIds` set, derived from MICRO_LOG on mount. Updated locally after each "I tried this" tap without requiring reload.

### 2. Inline mini-timer for timed body actions

**Currently:** Only `type: 'breath'` actions get timer buttons (1/3/5 min). The existing `startTimer()`, `timer` state, `running` state, and `fmtTime()` in `MicroActionPanel` are reused.

**Change:** Add optional `timer` field (seconds) to action data. For non-breath actions that have `timer`, render:

```
 Start [duration] timer
```

Button triggers `startTimer(action.timer)`. Countdown displays in `M:SS` format using existing `fmtTime`. When timer reaches 0, auto-mark action as completed (call logAction with completed: true).

**Actions receiving timers:**

| ID | Action | Timer |
|----|--------|-------|
| s1 | Comfort object | 120s |
| a1 | Clench and release | 15s |
| w1 | Permission to pause | 300s |
| x3 | Progressive release | 120s |

**UI:** Rendered inside the `body` and `grounding` type detail panels, below the description text. Same visual style as existing breath timer buttons but single button (not 1/3/5 options).

### 3. Breathing tool link

**Location:** Inside `type: 'breath'` detail panels, below the existing timer buttons.

**Markup:**
```
Or try the full experience ->
[Open Breathing tool]  (gs-btn-ghost style link)
```

**Routing:** Calls `onNavigate('breathing')`.

**Prop change:** `MicroActionPanel` gains an `onNavigate` prop. Passed through from `ModuleScreen` which already receives navigation context. Falls back gracefully - if `onNavigate` is not provided, the link is not rendered.

### 4. Patterns tab - "This week you tried" card

**Location:** `DashboardScreen`, new card between "This Week" summary and "Pattern Insights".

**Data source:** `STORAGE.MICRO_LOG` entries from past 7 days where `completed === true`.

**Rendering logic:**
1. Load MICRO_LOG, filter to entries with `completed === true` and timestamp within last 7 days
2. Resolve `action` field -> title by looking up across all MICRO_ACTIONS emotion arrays
3. Deduplicate by action ID (same action tried multiple times -> shown once)
4. If completed actions exist: **" This week you tried"** heading, followed by action titles as a gentle list (emoji + title per line, max 8 shown)
5. If no completed actions: **"Try a Quick Action from any lesson - it'll show up here."** in muted text, no card chrome

**Empty state integration:** This card is shown regardless of whether check-ins exist. It provides a sense of progress even for users who only use Quick Actions without formal journaling.

### 5. Expand to 5 actions per emotion

Add 2 new actions to each of the 8 emotion categories (16 new actions, 40 total). New actions follow the existing schema with optional `timer` field.

**New actions by emotion:**

**numb:**
- `n4`  Deep sighing - "Take 3 deep sighs. Let each one be louder than the last." (type: breath)
- `n5`  Texture hunt - "Find 3 different textures nearby. Touch each one for 10 seconds." (type: grounding, timer: 30)

**angry:**
- `a4`  Stomp walk - "Walk hard and fast for 2 minutes. Let your feet hit the ground with purpose." (type: body, timer: 120)
- `a5`  Cold reset - "Hold ice or run cold water on your wrists. The shock resets your nervous system." (type: grounding)

**sad:**
- `s4`  Self-hug - "Wrap your arms around yourself and squeeze gently for 30 seconds. This activates your calm system." (type: body, timer: 30)
- `s5`  Candle gaze - "Light a candle or look at a warm light. Watch it quietly for 2 minutes." (type: grounding, timer: 120)

**lost:**
- `l4`  Window pause - "Go to a window. Look at something far away for 1 minute. Let your eyes rest." (type: grounding, timer: 60)
- `l5`  Heart anchor - "Place your hand on your chest. Count 10 heartbeats. You are here." (type: body)

**guilty:**
- `g4`  Permission slip - "Write yourself a permission slip: 'I give myself permission to ___.' Fill in the blank." (type: expression)
- `g5`  Wave breathing - "Inhale for 4, exhale for 6. Imagine waves washing guilt out to sea." (type: breath)

**anxious:**
- `x4`  Palm press - "Press your palms together hard for 10 seconds, then release. Notice the tingle." (type: body, timer: 10)
- `x5`  Countdown - "Count backward from 20 slowly. Focus on each number." (type: grounding)

**lonely:**
- `o4`  Memory scroll - "Look at 3 photos of people you love. Let yourself smile or cry." (type: expression)
- `o5`  Voice comfort - "Listen to a voicemail, podcast, or song by someone whose voice feels safe." (type: expression)

**overwhelmed:**
- `w4`  Cold face - "Splash cold water on your face or hold a cold cloth to your forehead for 30 seconds." (type: body, timer: 30)
- `w5`  One box - "Pick one small area (a drawer, a shelf). Organize just that. Nothing else." (type: grounding)

## Data Changes

### MICRO_ACTIONS schema

```javascript
// Existing
{ id: string, emoji: string, title: string, desc: string, type: 'grounding'|'breath'|'body'|'expression' }

// New (optional timer field)
{ id: string, emoji: string, title: string, desc: string, type: string, timer?: number }
```

Backward compatible - existing actions without `timer` are unaffected.

### MICRO_LOG schema

```javascript
// Existing entries
{ id: string, emotion: string, action: string, timestamp: ISO }

// New entries add completed flag
{ id: string, emotion: string, action: string, timestamp: ISO, completed: true }
```

Backward compatible - old entries without `completed` are treated as views/opens (not completions). The `completed` field is only set to `true` (never `false`), so its absence means "not completed."

### No new storage keys

Everything uses the existing `STORAGE.MICRO_LOG` key.

## CSS Changes

New classes in `griefspace-app.html`:

- `.gs-micro-done-btn` - "I tried this" button (outline style, muted)
- `.gs-micro-done-confirm` - "Good. That matters." confirmation text (fade-in animation)
- `.gs-micro-done-settled` - "[yes] Done" settled state (muted checkmark)
- `.gs-micro-badge` - small checkmark badge on completed action cards
- `.gs-micro-timer-btn` - " Start timer" button for body/grounding actions
- `.gs-micro-breathing-link` - "Open Breathing tool" ghost link
- `.gs-tried-card` - "This week you tried" card in Patterns dashboard
- `.gs-tried-list` - list of tried action items

## Component Changes

| Component | Change |
|-----------|--------|
| `MicroActionPanel` | Add `onNavigate` prop, `completedIds` state, "I tried this" button, timer for non-breath timed actions, breathing tool link |
| `ModuleScreen` | Pass `onNavigate` through to `MicroActionPanel` |
| `DashboardScreen` | Add "This week you tried" card reading from MICRO_LOG |
| `MICRO_ACTIONS` (data) | Add 16 new actions (2 per emotion), add `timer` field to 8 actions |

## Testing

QA harness additions:
- Verify MICRO_ACTIONS has 5 entries per emotion (40 total)
- Verify `timer` field is a positive number where present
- Verify MICRO_LOG entries with `completed: true` are created by logAction
- Verify DashboardScreen renders tried-actions card when MICRO_LOG has completed entries


---

## Source: `07_Evidence_and_Reading/Landmark_Library/_source/50-Paper-Briefs.md`

# The Landmark Psychiatry Teaching Library
## 50 Brief Clinical Summaries (The "2-Minute Read" Companion)

*Generated 2026-04-11. Companion to the full-length NotebookLM Audio Overviews. Each brief is ~200 words and is designed to be (a) read in two minutes as a rounds-ready refresher, (b) used as a printable rotation handout, and (c) piped into NotebookLM as a Customize prompt to generate a 3-4 minute Brief Audio Overview for each paper.*

*Voice: attending teaching a resident on the walk to the coffee machine. Honest about limitations. Numeric where the numbers are known.*

---

## How to Use This File

Each entry has three parts:
1. **Citation block** - exact reference + the short filename used in `01-Individual-Papers/`
2. **The Brief** - 180-220 words of standalone prose structured as *context -> design -> result -> Monday-morning implication -> caveat*
3. **NotebookLM Customize Prompt** - paste this into NotebookLM's "Customize" field when generating the Brief Audio Overview version of this paper

---

# PSYCHOPHARMACOLOGY

## 1. CATIE - Lieberman 2005

**Citation:** Lieberman JA, Stroup TS, McEvoy JP, et al. Effectiveness of antipsychotic drugs in patients with chronic schizophrenia. *N Engl J Med.* 2005;353(12):1209-1223.
**File:** `01-CATIE-Lieberman-2005.m4a`

### The Brief
Before CATIE, the field widely believed second-generation antipsychotics were meaningfully more effective and safer than the older drugs - a belief built mostly on industry trials in highly selected populations. NIMH funded CATIE to test that belief in people who actually show up to a community clinic. Lieberman and colleagues randomized 1,493 patients with chronic schizophrenia to olanzapine, quetiapine, risperidone, ziprasidone, or the mid-potency first-generation drug perphenazine, double-blind, for up to 18 months. The primary outcome was all-cause discontinuation - a brutal but honest composite of efficacy, tolerability, and patient preference. Seventy-four percent of patients stopped their assigned drug before the trial ended. Olanzapine had the longest time to discontinuation, but delivered the worst weight and metabolic burden. The other three SGAs were statistically indistinguishable from perphenazine. **Monday morning:** stop choosing antipsychotics by generation. Choose by metabolic risk, EPS vulnerability, sedation needs, adherence history, and prior response. Mid-potency first-generation drugs remain legitimate first-line options. **Caveat:** CATIE enrolled chronic, previously-treated patients; first-episode results look different, and clozapine only entered in Phase 2E.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on the CATIE trial (Lieberman 2005, NEJM). Structure: (1) the pre-CATIE assumption that SGAs were clearly superior, (2) the NIMH-funded pragmatic design, N=1,493, double-blind, 18 months, perphenazine as the FGA comparator, (3) the 74% overall discontinuation rate and what that composite outcome was measuring, (4) the specific finding that olanzapine won on efficacy but lost on metabolic burden while perphenazine held its own, (5) the Monday-morning implication: choose antipsychotics by side-effect profile, not generation. Attending-teaching-a-resident tone, no breathless praise. Close with the caveat that the sample was chronic and previously treated, not first-episode.

---

## 2. STAR*D - Rush 2006

**Citation:** Rush AJ, Trivedi MH, Wisniewski SR, et al. Acute and longer-term outcomes in depressed outpatients requiring one or several treatment steps. *Am J Psychiatry.* 2006;163(11):1905-1917.
**File:** `02-STARD-Rush-2006.m4a`

### The Brief
STAR*D is the trial that made the field honest about depression outcomes. Rush and colleagues enrolled 4,041 real-world outpatients with non-psychotic major depression - people with the comorbidities and social chaos that industry trials systematically exclude - and ran them through up to four sequential pharmacotherapy steps under measurement-based care. Step 1 was open-label citalopram. Non-remitters could be randomized in Step 2 to a switch (sertraline, bupropion-SR, venlafaxine-XR) or augmentation (bupropion-SR or buspirone added to citalopram). Steps 3 and 4 escalated further. Remission rates fell sharply with each step: roughly 33%, 25%, 18%, and 13%. Cumulative remission across four steps reached about 67%, but relapse rates climbed - 40% at one year after a Step 1 remission versus 71% after a Step 4 remission. **Monday morning:** use a validated scale (QIDS or PHQ-9) at every visit, push the first SSRI to an adequate dose and duration before declaring failure, and give patients a realistic expectation - roughly one in three will remit fully on drug number one. Don't call anyone "treatment-resistant" until at least two adequate trials. **Caveat:** no placebo arm. The pharmacologic signal is entangled with spontaneous improvement and placebo response.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on STAR*D (Rush 2006 AJP). Structure: (1) why a pragmatic sequential trial was needed after decades of industry-sponsored 8-week placebo trials, (2) the 4,041-patient design with measurement-based care and four sequential steps, (3) the headline numbers - 33%/25%/18%/13% step-wise remission, 67% cumulative, 40-71% one-year relapse depending on how many steps it took, (4) the Monday-morning implications: use PHQ-9 or QIDS at every visit, push doses, don't call someone "treatment-resistant" after one undertreated trial. Attending-teaching-a-resident tone. Close with the no-placebo-arm caveat.

---

## 3. STEP-BD - Sachs 2007

**Citation:** Sachs GS, Nierenberg AA, Calabrese JR, et al. Effectiveness of adjunctive antidepressant treatment for bipolar depression. *N Engl J Med.* 2007;356(17):1711-1722.
**File:** `03-STEP-BD-Sachs-2007.m4a`

### The Brief
For decades psychiatrists reflexively added an antidepressant when a bipolar patient became depressed, on the theory that it couldn't hurt. STEP-BD tested that assumption. Sachs and colleagues, within the NIMH Systematic Treatment Enhancement Program for Bipolar Disorder, randomized 366 patients with bipolar depression who were already on a mood stabilizer to adjunctive paroxetine, bupropion, or matching placebo for up to 26 weeks. The primary outcome was "durable recovery" - eight consecutive weeks of euthymia. The result was clean and disappointing: 23.5% on adjunctive antidepressant versus 27.3% on placebo achieved durable recovery. No benefit. And - importantly - no increased rate of treatment-emergent affective switch in the antidepressant arm, countering the other dogma that antidepressants reliably induce mania in bipolar patients. **Monday morning:** in bipolar depression, your first move should be to optimize the mood stabilizer (lithium, lamotrigine, quetiapine, lurasidone, cariprazine) rather than add an SSRI or SNRI. If you do add one, know you are not delivering much benefit on average. **Caveat:** this was a negative pragmatic trial in patients already on stabilizers; it doesn't rule out antidepressant benefit for specific subtypes, nor does it comment on bipolar I versus II or rapid cycling.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on STEP-BD Sachs 2007 NEJM. Structure: (1) the pre-trial reflex of adding antidepressants in bipolar depression, (2) the N=366 RCT design with paroxetine vs. bupropion vs. placebo added to mood stabilizer, (3) the negative result - 23.5% vs. 27.3% durable recovery, no meaningful benefit, (4) the reassuring secondary finding that switch rates did not differ, (5) the Monday-morning takeaway: optimize the mood stabilizer first, don't reflexively add an antidepressant. Attending-teaching-a-resident tone. Close with the caveat that STEP-BD doesn't settle every subgroup question.

---

## 4. Kane Clozapine - Kane 1988

**Citation:** Kane J, Honigfeld G, Singer J, Meltzer H. Clozapine for the treatment-resistant schizophrenic. A double-blind comparison with chlorpromazine. *Arch Gen Psychiatry.* 1988;45(9):789-796.
**File:** `04-Kane-Clozapine-1988.m4a`

### The Brief
Kane 1988 is the single trial that put clozapine back on the map after it was pulled from the US market in the 1970s over agranulocytosis deaths. Kane, Honigfeld, Singer, and Meltzer randomized 268 patients with schizophrenia who had failed at least three prior antipsychotic trials (plus a prospective haloperidol lead-in failure) to clozapine or chlorpromazine with benztropine, double-blind, for six weeks. The response rate was striking: 30% on clozapine versus 4% on chlorpromazine met the *a priori* response criterion (>=20% BPRS reduction plus a CGI of "mildly ill" or better). For a population the field had written off, a one-in-three response was revolutionary. This trial is the evidentiary foundation for every modern guideline that says clozapine is the treatment of choice for treatment-resistant schizophrenia - a standard that still applies almost 40 years later. **Monday morning:** if a patient has failed two adequate antipsychotic trials, clozapine is the next step, not the last resort. Monitoring logistics should not be the reason you delay. **Caveat:** short six-week trial, older comparator, and real-world clozapine uptake remains catastrophically low compared with what the evidence supports.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Kane et al 1988, Arch Gen Psych, clozapine for treatment-resistant schizophrenia. Structure: (1) the historical context - clozapine pulled from US market in 1970s over agranulocytosis, (2) the N=268 RCT design with three prior failures plus a prospective haloperidol lead-in, six weeks double-blind versus chlorpromazine, (3) the 30% vs. 4% response result and why that was revolutionary, (4) the Monday-morning implication: after two failed antipsychotic trials, clozapine is the next step, not a last resort. Attending-teaching-a-resident tone. Close with the caveat that real-world clozapine underuse remains a field-wide failure.

---

## 5. Cipriani Antidepressants Network Meta-Analysis - Cipriani 2018

**Citation:** Cipriani A, Furukawa TA, Salanti G, et al. Comparative efficacy and acceptability of 21 antidepressant drugs for the acute treatment of adults with major depressive disorder: a systematic review and network meta-analysis. *Lancet.* 2018;391(10128):1357-1366.
**File:** `05-Cipriani-AD-NMA-2018.m4a`

### The Brief
Cipriani 2018 is the definitive network meta-analysis of the antidepressant evidence base - 522 double-blind RCTs, 116,477 participants, 21 drugs - answering two blunt questions: do they work, and are any meaningfully better than the others. The answer to the first question is yes: every one of the 21 agents beat placebo on response (odds ratio 1.37-2.13). The answer to the second is less satisfying. Amitriptyline, mirtazapine, escitalopram, venlafaxine, and paroxetine came out on top for efficacy. Agomelatine, citalopram, escitalopram, fluoxetine, sertraline, and vortioxetine were best tolerated. Amitriptyline won efficacy but lost tolerability. The league tables got the headlines, but the real lesson is that between-drug differences in efficacy are modest compared to the difference any drug makes over placebo, and tolerability drives real-world effectiveness more than head-to-head efficacy rankings. **Monday morning:** reach for escitalopram, sertraline, or mirtazapine as reasonable first-line SSRIs/SNRIs; reserve amitriptyline for patients willing to accept the anticholinergic burden; use the league tables as tiebreakers, not as commandments. **Caveat:** acute-phase data only, 8-week outcomes, publication bias imperfectly corrected, and the CINeMA confidence ratings were "moderate" to "very low" for most comparisons.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Cipriani et al 2018 Lancet, the 21-antidepressant network meta-analysis. # MOOD DISORDERS

## 6. BALANCE - Geddes 2010

**Citation:** BALANCE investigators and collaborators; Geddes JR, Goodwin GM, Rendell J, et al. Lithium plus valproate combination therapy versus monotherapy for relapse prevention in bipolar I disorder (BALANCE): an open-label randomised trial. *Lancet.* 2010;375(9712):385-395.
**File:** `06-BALANCE-Geddes-2010.m4a`

### The Brief
Before BALANCE, psychiatrists routinely used lithium plus valproate combinations for bipolar I maintenance on the assumption that two mood stabilizers must be better than one - despite no head-to-head trial supporting the combo. BALANCE put the three competing strategies against each other. Geddes and colleagues randomized 330 patients with bipolar I disorder to open-label lithium monotherapy, valproate monotherapy, or the combination, and followed them for up to two years. The primary outcome was initiation of new treatment for a mood episode. Combination therapy and lithium monotherapy both outperformed valproate monotherapy: hazard ratio 0.59 for combination versus valproate (95% CI 0.42-0.83) and 0.71 for lithium versus valproate (0.51-1.00). Lithium and the combination did not differ significantly from each other. The trial restored lithium's status as the gold standard for bipolar I maintenance and clarified that valproate monotherapy is the weakest of the three options. **Monday morning:** in bipolar I maintenance, lithium should be your default first-line choice. Valproate monotherapy is defensible in lithium-intolerant patients but is the weakest option on average. Combination therapy is reasonable for patients with breakthrough episodes on monotherapy. **Caveat:** open-label trial, bipolar I only, and tolerability differences complicate comparisons.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on BALANCE (Geddes et al 2010 Lancet). Structure: (1) the pre-trial assumption that combo lithium + valproate was superior for maintenance, (2) the N=330 open-label RCT with three arms (lithium, valproate, combo), up to 2-year follow-up, (3) the result - combo and lithium both beat valproate monotherapy (HR 0.59 and 0.71), combo did not beat lithium alone, (4) the Monday-morning takeaway: lithium is first-line maintenance for bipolar I, valproate monotherapy is the weakest of the three, combo is for breakthrough. Attending tone. Close with the open-label caveat and the bipolar-I-only scope.

## 7. Cipriani Lithium & Suicide - Cipriani 2013

**Citation:** Cipriani A, Hawton K, Stockton S, Geddes JR. Lithium in the prevention of suicide in mood disorders: updated systematic review and meta-analysis. *BMJ.* 2013;346:f3646.
**File:** `07-Cipriani-Lithium-Suicide-2013.m4a`

### The Brief
Lithium's antisuicide effect is one of the cleanest signals in all of psychiatry, and Cipriani 2013 is the meta-analysis that crystallized it. Cipriani, Hawton, Stockton, and Geddes pooled 48 randomized controlled trials (6,674 patients) comparing lithium with placebo or other active drugs in mood disorders, looking at suicide and deliberate self-harm as primary outcomes. Against placebo, lithium reduced the risk of suicide by roughly 60% (odds ratio 0.13, 95% CI 0.03-0.66) and all-cause mortality by about 38% (OR 0.38, 0.15-0.95). Against other active drugs (including anticonvulsants), lithium also reduced suicide risk. The mechanism is still debated - some combination of impulse control, direct mood stabilization, and possibly specific effects on aggression - but the clinical signal is consistent across trials. **Monday morning:** in any patient with bipolar disorder or recurrent unipolar depression with a history of suicidal behavior or elevated suicide risk, lithium deserves explicit consideration regardless of whether the mood syndrome is textbook. Framing lithium as "the mood stabilizer with an anti-suicide effect" shifts the risk-benefit conversation meaningfully. **Caveat:** the number of suicide events in any individual trial is small, confidence intervals are wide, and lithium's narrow therapeutic window and long-term renal risks must be weighed against the benefit.

Attending tone. Close with caveats about small event counts, wide CIs, and lithium's therapeutic window.

## 8. Leucht Antipsychotics Network Meta-Analysis - Leucht 2013

**Citation:** Leucht S, Cipriani A, Spineli L, et al. Comparative efficacy and tolerability of 15 antipsychotic drugs in schizophrenia: a multiple-treatments meta-analysis. *Lancet.* 2013;382(9896):951-962.
**French equivalent of Cipriani 2018 for antipsychotics.*
**File:** `08-Leucht-AP-NMA-2013.m4a`

### The Brief
Leucht 2013 is to antipsychotics what Cipriani 2018 is to antidepressants: a network meta-analysis that lets you compare 15 drugs directly and indirectly from 212 RCTs (43,049 patients) with acute schizophrenia. The efficacy ordering surprised almost no one who had read the literature carefully: clozapine sits well ahead of the pack (effect size on PANSS/BPRS around -0.88 versus placebo). Amisulpride, olanzapine, and risperidone form a second tier. Haloperidol, quetiapine, aripiprazole, sertindole, ziprasidone, chlorpromazine, asenapine, lurasidone, and iloperidone cluster below. The differences within the second and third tiers are modest; the difference between clozapine and everything else is real. Tolerability profiles diverge sharply - olanzapine is the worst for weight, haloperidol the worst for EPS, clozapine the worst for sedation and everything else except EPS. **Monday morning:** when acute efficacy is the dominant concern in treatment-resistant disease, clozapine is the right answer. Among non-clozapine options, differences are small enough that side-effect profile and prior response should dominate the choice. **Caveat:** acute-phase trials (6-week horizon), industry-sponsored trials dominate the evidence base, and the league tables are less informative than they look because within-tier differences are smaller than their confidence intervals.

Attending tone. Close with caveats about acute-phase horizon and industry-sponsored evidence base.

## 9. Kellner Continuation ECT - Kellner 2006

**Citation:** Kellner CH, Knapp RG, Petrides G, et al. Continuation electroconvulsive therapy vs pharmacotherapy for relapse prevention in major depression: a multisite study from the Consortium for Research in Electroconvulsive Therapy (CORE). *Arch Gen Psychiatry.* 2006;63(12):1337-1344.
**File:** `09-Kellner-ECT-2006.m4a`

### The Brief
ECT has the highest acute response rate of any treatment in major depression - roughly 70-90% - but relapse after acute-course ECT is catastrophic without continuation treatment. The CORE continuation ECT trial tested the two realistic options for patients who had remitted after an acute ECT course. Kellner and the CORE investigators enrolled 531 depressed patients, delivered an open-label acute ECT course, and randomized the 184 remitters to either (a) continuation ECT on a fixed tapering schedule or (b) continuation pharmacotherapy with lithium plus nortriptyline. Follow-up was six months. Six-month relapse rates were statistically indistinguishable: 37.1% for continuation ECT and 31.6% for combination pharmacotherapy - both better than the ~84% relapse observed historically with no continuation treatment. **Monday morning:** if you send a patient for acute ECT, you must have a continuation plan before they finish - either scheduled continuation ECT or aggressive combination pharmacotherapy (lithium-plus-TCA is the evidence-based default; lithium plus venlafaxine is a reasonable modernization). Untreated, two-thirds will relapse within six months. **Caveat:** both arms still had substantial relapse rates; more aggressive maintenance strategies (continuation ECT plus pharmacotherapy together) are worth considering for very high-risk patients.

Structure: (1) the problem of catastrophic relapse after acute ECT without a continuation plan, (2) the CORE design - 531 enrolled, 184 remitters randomized to continuation ECT vs. lithium+nortriptyline, 6-month follow-up, (3) the result: 37% vs. 32% relapse, not statistically different, both far better than historical untreated 84%, (4) the Monday-morning takeaway: every acute ECT course needs a specific, evidence-based continuation plan. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that even these arms had substantial relapse and combined strategies may be needed for highest-risk patients.

## 10. Moncrieff Serotonin Umbrella Review - Moncrieff 2022

**Citation:** Moncrieff J, Cooper RE, Stockmann T, Amendola S, Hengartner MP, Horowitz MA. The serotonin theory of depression: a systematic umbrella review of the evidence. *Mol Psychiatry.* 2022;28(8):3243-3256.
**File:** `10-Moncrieff-Discontinuation-2019.m4a` *(existing filename retained for continuity)*

### The Brief
The "chemical imbalance" story - that depression is caused by low serotonin and SSRIs correct it - was one of the most successful popularizations of psychiatric science, and one of the least defensible. Moncrieff and colleagues conducted an umbrella review, synthesizing 17 systematic reviews and meta-analyses across six research lines: CSF 5-HIAA, serotonin and metabolite levels in plasma/platelets, 5-HT1A receptor binding, serotonin transporter (SERT) imaging, tryptophan depletion studies, and SLC6A4 gene association and interaction studies. Across all six lines, the review found no consistent evidence that depressed patients differ from controls in central serotonin or serotonergic activity in a direction that would support the low-serotonin model. The paper does *not* argue that SSRIs don't work - a separate question with its own mixed evidence base - but it does argue that the explanatory story patients and clinicians have been telling each other for thirty years is not supported by the empirical record. **Monday morning:** stop telling patients they have a "chemical imbalance." The honest explanation is that we don't fully understand how SSRIs work, but that they reliably outperform placebo for moderate-to-severe depression and that the mechanism is probably more about neuroplasticity and downstream effects than a simple serotonin deficit. **Caveat:** an umbrella review inherits the methodological limits of its constituent reviews, and absence of consistent evidence is not evidence of absence.

Structure: (1) the cultural power of the "chemical imbalance" story and why a synthesis of the serotonin evidence was needed, (2) the umbrella review design covering 17 meta-analyses across six research lines (CSF 5-HIAA, plasma/platelet serotonin, 5-HT1A, SERT imaging, tryptophan depletion, SLC6A4 genetics), (3) the finding: no consistent evidence supporting the low-serotonin model, (4) the careful distinction: this does *not* prove SSRIs don't work; it disproves a simplistic explanatory story, (5) the Monday-morning implication: stop saying "chemical imbalance" to patients; use honest language about uncertainty and plasticity. Attending tone. Close with the caveat about umbrella review limits and absence-of-evidence versus evidence-of-absence.

# FAMILY AND SYSTEMS

## 11. Brown Expressed Emotion - Brown 1962

**Citation:** Brown GW, Monck EM, Carstairs GM, Wing JK. Influence of family life on the course of schizophrenic illness. *Br J Prev Soc Med.* 1962;16(2):55-68.
**File:** `11-Brown-EE-1962.m4a`

### The Brief
This is the paper that made the family environment measurable in schizophrenia research and seeded fifty years of family-intervention science. Brown and colleagues followed 128 men with schizophrenia discharged from hospital to different types of households - parental home, marital home, lodgings - and tracked relapse at one year. The striking finding was that patients returning to homes with high levels of expressed emotion (critical comments, hostility, emotional over-involvement) relapsed at roughly double the rate of those returning to low-EE environments. The methodology was agnostic to diagnosis and specific etiology; what mattered was the measurable emotional temperature of the household. This was the origin of the "high-EE" construct that would later be operationalized in the Camberwell Family Interview and reproduced in dozens of studies across cultures. **Monday morning:** when you're planning discharge from an inpatient unit, the receiving environment is a modifiable relapse risk factor. Ask about criticism, over-involvement, and household friction - don't assume "home" means "safe." **Caveat:** small sample, all-male, 1950s UK context. The original numbers have been refined many times; the conceptual contribution is the durable landmark, not the specific percentages.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Brown et al 1962, the Expressed Emotion paper. Structure: (1) the state of schizophrenia science in the 1950s and the absence of any measurable social/environmental predictor of relapse, (2) the prospective cohort design, N=128 men discharged from hospital, followed one year, (3) the finding that high-EE households produced roughly double the relapse rate, (4) the methodological legacy - the Camberwell Family Interview and decades of replication, (5) the Monday-morning implication: discharge planning must include a read on the household emotional climate. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that the specific 1962 numbers are dated but the construct is durable.

## 12. Leff Family Intervention - Leff 1982

**Citation:** Leff J, Kuipers L, Berkowitz R, Eberlein-Vries R, Sturgeon D. A controlled trial of social intervention in the families of schizophrenic patients. *Br J Psychiatry.* 1982;141:121-134.
**File:** `12-Leff-Family-Intervention-1982.m4a`

### The Brief
Twenty years after Brown discovered that high expressed emotion predicted relapse, the question became: if we intervene in the family, does the relapse curve bend? Leff and colleagues answered it with the first randomized controlled trial of family intervention in schizophrenia. They recruited 24 patients living with high-EE relatives, on antipsychotic medication, and randomized them to routine outpatient care versus a structured package combining relatives' education, a relatives' group, and family sessions aimed at lowering criticism and over-involvement. Over nine months of follow-up, relapse was 50% in the control group versus 9% in the intervention group - a difference that was both statistically and clinically striking for a sample of 24. The mechanism appeared to be the intended one: EE measurably dropped in intervention families, and the patients whose family EE dropped were the ones who stayed well. **Monday morning:** family psychoeducation and EE-lowering work are not soft add-ons to schizophrenia care; they belong in every discharge plan where the patient is returning to a high-EE household. **Caveat:** small sample, single-site, requires trained facilitators, and the 9% figure is almost certainly too rosy for generic community implementation - but the direction of effect has been replicated many times (see Pharoah Cochrane, paper #20).

Structure: (1) the problem Brown's EE finding left open - does intervening in families bend the relapse curve, (2) the design: N=24, all with high-EE relatives, randomized to usual care vs. a structured package of education + relatives' group + family sessions, nine-month follow-up, (3) the headline result - 50% vs. 9% relapse - and the mechanism finding that EE measurably dropped in intervention families, (4) the Monday-morning implication: family psychoeducation belongs in every discharge plan for patients returning to high-EE households. Attending tone. Close with caveats about small N, single site, and the optimistic effect size.

## 13. Falloon Family Management - Falloon 1982

**Citation:** Falloon IR, Boyd JL, McGill CW, et al. Family management in the prevention of exacerbations of schizophrenia: a controlled study. *N Engl J Med.* 1982;306(24):1437-1440.
**File:** `13-Falloon-Family-Therapy-1982.m4a`

### The Brief
Falloon's NEJM paper - published the same year as Leff's BJP trial - is the second pillar of the family-intervention evidence base in schizophrenia. Falloon and colleagues randomized 36 patients with schizophrenia who were living with families to either individual management (clinic-based medication management plus supportive individual therapy) or behavioral family management delivered in the home, emphasizing communication training, structured problem-solving, and education about the illness. Both groups received optimal medication. At nine months, the family-therapy group had a 6% exacerbation rate versus 44% in the individual group. Hospitalization rates followed the same pattern. The clinical significance is amplified by the venue: a NEJM paper in 1982 legitimized family therapy for schizophrenia in mainstream medical thinking and shifted the field from "family therapy as adjunct" to "family therapy as core." **Monday morning:** structured, behavioral, problem-solving family work is as evidence-based as any antipsychotic choice in community-dwelling patients with schizophrenia. Delivery in the home is part of the recipe, not an optional luxury. **Caveat:** small sample, specialized therapist training, and the outsized effect sizes of early family-intervention trials have softened as studies scaled up (see Pharoah Cochrane).

## 14. McFarlane Multifamily Groups - McFarlane 1995

**Citation:** McFarlane WR, Lukens E, Link B, et al. Multiple-family groups and psychoeducation in the treatment of schizophrenia. *Arch Gen Psychiatry.* 1995;52(8):679-687.
**File:** `14-McFarlane-Multifamily-1995.m4a`

### The Brief
Single-family therapy is effective (see papers #12 and #13) but resource-intensive. McFarlane's innovation was to deliver the same core psychoeducational and problem-solving content to groups of five or six families meeting together. The 1995 trial compared multifamily group psychoeducation to single-family psychoeducation in 172 patients with schizophrenia over two years. Multifamily groups produced significantly lower relapse rates - 16% versus 27% at two years - and the effect was driven most strongly in patients with the highest baseline symptom burden and the most impaired social networks. The hypothesized mechanism is social: putting multiple families in one room extends the support network, normalizes the illness, reduces stigma, and gives families a chance to model effective coping for each other in a way that a solo family room cannot. **Monday morning:** if you run a clinic that treats serious mental illness, a multifamily group is higher-yield-per-clinician-hour than a single-family model and should be part of the service mix. **Caveat:** multifamily groups require specific training to facilitate well, and the treatment gain depends on reasonably consistent family attendance across sessions.

## 15. Miklowitz Family-Focused Therapy for Bipolar - Miklowitz 2003

**Citation:** Miklowitz DJ, George EL, Richards JA, Simoneau TL, Suddath RL. A randomized study of family-focused psychoeducation and pharmacotherapy in the outpatient management of bipolar disorder. *Arch Gen Psychiatry.* 2003;60(9):904-912.
**File:** `15-Miklowitz-FFT-Bipolar-2003.m4a`

### The Brief
Family intervention worked in schizophrenia - did it generalize to bipolar disorder? Miklowitz and colleagues ran the trial that answered yes. They randomized 101 recently-ill bipolar I patients, all on pharmacotherapy, to either (a) a 21-session Family-Focused Treatment (FFT) package - psychoeducation, communication enhancement training, problem-solving skills - or (b) a briefer two-session crisis-management condition. Patients were followed for two years. FFT recipients had fewer relapses (35% vs. 54%), longer time to relapse, less severe mood symptoms, and better medication adherence. The effect size for depressive symptoms was larger than for manic symptoms, a pattern that has held up in subsequent FFT trials. **Monday morning:** for a bipolar I patient discharging from a mood episode, adding a structured 21-session family-focused psychoeducation course to medication management meaningfully reduces relapse - especially depressive relapse. FFT is manualized and trainable; if your clinic doesn't offer it, at minimum deliver the psychoeducation and communication components. **Caveat:** the control arm was deliberately thin (two crisis sessions), so part of the effect reflects the dose of therapist contact, not just the FFT content itself; head-to-head trials against other intensive psychotherapies show smaller differences.

## 16. Diamond Attachment-Based Family Therapy - Diamond 2010

**Citation:** Diamond GS, Wintersteen MB, Brown GK, Diamond GM, Gallop R, Shelef K, Levy S. Attachment-based family therapy for adolescents with suicidal ideation: a randomized controlled trial. *J Am Acad Child Adolesc Psychiatry.* 2010;49(2):122-131.
**File:** `16-Diamond-ABFT-2010.m4a`

### The Brief
Adolescent suicidal ideation is high-prevalence, high-stakes, and poorly served by most of our treatment literature, which tends to focus on adult populations and acute post-attempt care. Diamond and colleagues developed Attachment-Based Family Therapy (ABFT) - a 12-week manualized treatment derived from attachment theory that works directly on the parent-adolescent bond as the therapeutic target, using specific tasks to re-open conversations that have broken down in the family. They randomized 66 adolescents (ages 12-17) with moderate-to-severe suicidal ideation to ABFT versus enhanced usual care. At 12 weeks, 87% of ABFT adolescents had a clinically significant reduction in suicidal ideation versus 52% in the comparison condition. Depressive symptoms also improved more with ABFT. The effects held at 24-week follow-up. The trial is modest in size but important because it shows that a family-based intervention can move the needle specifically on suicidal ideation in teens, not just generic depressive symptoms. **Monday morning:** for an adolescent with suicidal ideation living in a conflicted or disengaged family, an attachment-focused family intervention is a reasonable evidence-based option - especially as an alternative or complement to individual CBT/DBT. **Caveat:** small sample, moderate-severity rather than actively attempting adolescents, and the effect has not been tested at scale.

## 17. Pinsof Family Therapy Efficacy - Pinsof & Wynne 1995

**Citation:** Pinsof WM, Wynne LC. The efficacy of marital and family therapy: an empirical overview, conclusions, and recommendations. *J Marital Fam Ther.* 1995;21(4):585-613.
**File:** `17-Pinsof-Science-FT-2018.m4a` *(existing filename retained)*

### The Brief
By the mid-1990s the literature on marital and family therapy was large, scattered, and dominated by small trials with heterogeneous interventions. Pinsof and Wynne led a systematic empirical overview commissioned by the American Association for Marriage and Family Therapy to synthesize what the evidence actually supported. Their conclusion across dozens of controlled trials was blunt and useful: marital and family therapy is efficacious for specific conditions when matched to specific approaches - behavioral marital therapy for couple distress, structural family therapy and behavioral parent training for child conduct problems, family psychoeducation for schizophrenia and bipolar disorder, Multisystemic Therapy for juvenile delinquency, and emerging evidence for adolescent substance abuse. They were equally blunt about the limits: generic "family therapy" is not an evidence-based treatment in the same way that DBT or CBT is; the evidence supports specific, manualized approaches for specific presentations. **Monday morning:** when you refer a patient for "family therapy," name the problem and name the model. "Behavioral parent training for conduct disorder" is evidence-based; "family therapy for anything" is not. Ask the therapist what manual they are working from. **Caveat:** this is a 1995 synthesis - the field has grown substantially, but the central point (specific models for specific problems) has strengthened, not weakened.

## 18. Minuchin Psychosomatic Families - Minuchin 1975

**Citation:** Minuchin S, Baker L, Rosman BL, Liebman R, Milman L, Todd TC. A conceptual model of psychosomatic illness in children. Family organization and family therapy. *Arch Gen Psychiatry.* 1975;32(8):1031-1038.
**File:** `18-Minuchin-Psychosomatic-1975.m4a`

### The Brief
Minuchin's psychosomatic families paper is the foundation of structural family therapy as applied to medical illness, and one of the most influential conceptual papers in the family therapy literature. Minuchin and colleagues described a characteristic family pattern they observed in children with brittle diabetes, intractable asthma, and anorexia nervosa: enmeshment, overprotection, rigidity, conflict avoidance, and the recruitment of the child's symptoms into the regulation of family tensions. They argued that the child's physiological symptoms served a systemic function - stabilizing an otherwise unstable family homeostasis. The paper describes structural family therapy interventions aimed at restructuring transactional patterns (drawing boundaries between parental and child subsystems, surfacing and metabolizing marital conflict, blocking symptom-mediated detouring) and reports clinical improvement in a case series of children with brittle diabetes. **Monday morning:** in pediatric psychosomatic presentations that recur despite good medical management, think structurally - who benefits from the symptom? What conflicts does the symptom keep off the table? Name the patterns out loud with the family. Refer to a structural or strategic family therapist when the pattern is entrenched. **Caveat:** the empirical base for the "psychosomatic family" construct is a mix of case series and small studies; causal claims have been challenged by subsequent research. The conceptual contribution endures even where the specific etiologic claims have softened.

## 19. Leff Couple Therapy for Depression - Leff 2000

**Citation:** Leff J, Vearnals S, Brewin CR, et al. The London Depression Intervention Trial. Randomised controlled trial of antidepressants v. couple therapy in the treatment and maintenance of people with depression living with a partner: clinical outcome and costs. *Br J Psychiatry.* 2000;177:95-100.
**File:** `19-Leff-Couple-Therapy-2000.m4a`

### The Brief
Most depression trials treat the patient as if they live alone. The London Depression Intervention Trial tested an alternative premise: for a depressed patient living with a critical partner, couple therapy might outperform an antidepressant. Leff and colleagues randomized 77 depressed patients who were living with a critical partner (by Camberwell Family Interview criteria) to 12 to 20 sessions of systemic couple therapy versus antidepressant medication, with follow-up at one and two years. Couple therapy was significantly more acceptable - the dropout rate was dramatically lower (15% vs. 57%). Clinically, couple therapy produced greater reductions in depressive symptoms on the Beck Depression Inventory at the end of treatment and at two-year follow-up. Cost analyses showed couple therapy was not more expensive overall once dropouts and rehospitalizations were accounted for. **Monday morning:** when a depressed patient is in a distressed or critical relationship, couple therapy is not a second-line alternative to medication - for some patients it is a reasonable first-line treatment with better adherence and comparable outcomes. **Caveat:** small sample, single-city (London), and the design selected specifically for patients with a critical partner; results do not generalize to all depressed patients in relationships.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt

Structure: (1) the unusual premise: depressed patients living with critical partners might do better with couple therapy than medication, (2) the design: N=77, all with critical partners by Camberwell Family Interview, 12-20 sessions of systemic couple therapy vs. antidepressant, one- and two-year follow-up, (3) the result: much better acceptability (15% vs. 57% dropout), greater BDI improvement in couple therapy, comparable costs, (4) the Monday-morning implication: for depressed patients in distressed relationships, couple therapy is a legitimate first-line option. Attending tone. Close with caveats about small sample and selection for critical partners.

## 20. Pharoah Cochrane Family Intervention - Pharoah 2010

**Citation:** Pharoah F, Mari JJ, Rathbone J, Wong W. Family intervention for schizophrenia. *Cochrane Database Syst Rev.* 2010;(12):CD000088.
**File:** `20-Pharoah-Cochrane-FI-2010.m4a`

### The Brief
Pharoah's Cochrane review is the definitive quantitative synthesis of the family-intervention literature in schizophrenia. Pharoah and colleagues pooled 53 randomized controlled trials covering thousands of participants over three decades, comparing family intervention (most commonly Falloon- or Leff-style psychoeducation, communication training, and problem-solving delivered over months) against standard care. The meta-analytic findings align with the individual trial signals but with tighter precision: family intervention reduced relapse (roughly 26% relative risk reduction, RR 0.74), reduced hospital admissions, and improved medication compliance. Effects on social functioning and employment were smaller and less consistent. The review is also candid about methodological limits - blinding is impossible in behavioral trials, heterogeneity in "family intervention" as a construct is substantial, and publication bias cannot be ruled out - but the direction and robustness of the effect are beyond reasonable dispute. **Monday morning:** family intervention belongs in the core treatment menu for any community-dwelling patient with schizophrenia. If your service does not offer it, it should. **Caveat:** the effect sizes are real but smaller than the original Leff and Falloon trials suggested, and the generic "family intervention" label obscures meaningful differences in content, dose, and facilitator training.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Pharoah et al 2010 Cochrane, family intervention for schizophrenia. Structure: (1) why a Cochrane synthesis was needed after thirty years of small trials, (2) the scope: 53 RCTs, thousands of participants, family intervention vs. standard care, (3) the findings: ~26% relative risk reduction in relapse (RR 0.74), fewer admissions, better compliance, smaller effects on social functioning, (4) the Monday-morning implication: family intervention belongs in the core treatment menu for community-dwelling schizophrenia, (5) the methodological honesty about heterogeneity and smaller effect sizes than the original trials. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that "family intervention" is a heterogeneous label.

# PSYCHOTHERAPY

## 21. Linehan DBT - Linehan 1991

**Citation:** Linehan MM, Armstrong HE, Suarez A, Allmon D, Heard HL. Cognitive-behavioral treatment of chronically parasuicidal borderline patients. *Arch Gen Psychiatry.* 1991;48(12):1060-1064.
**File:** `21-Linehan-DBT-1991.m4a`

### The Brief
Before Linehan, borderline personality disorder was considered functionally untreatable - the received wisdom in psychiatry was that these patients cycled through ERs, burned out clinicians, and had to be endured rather than treated. Linehan published the first randomized controlled trial of Dialectical Behavior Therapy, which she had developed specifically for chronically parasuicidal patients with BPD. She randomized 44 women with BPD and a history of recurrent self-harm or suicide attempts to one year of DBT (weekly individual therapy plus weekly skills group plus phone coaching) versus treatment-as-usual in the community. At one year, DBT patients had significantly fewer and less severe parasuicidal acts, fewer days of inpatient hospitalization, and markedly better treatment retention (83% vs. 42%). Emotional and interpersonal outcome measures were less dramatic but trended in the same direction. The trial reframed BPD from a diagnosis of therapeutic nihilism to a diagnosis with a specific evidence-based treatment. **Monday morning:** a patient with BPD and recurrent self-harm should be referred to a program offering full-model DBT (individual + group + coaching + consultation team), not generic "therapy" - and if DBT is unavailable, at least the skills group and the hierarchical targeting approach should be part of the care plan. **Caveat:** small sample, all women, and the specific effect on self-harm is more robust than the effects on depressive symptoms or interpersonal functioning.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Linehan et al 1991, the first DBT RCT. Structure: (1) the pre-1991 view of BPD as essentially untreatable, (2) the design: N=44 women with BPD and recurrent parasuicide, randomized to one year of full-model DBT vs. community treatment-as-usual, (3) the results: fewer and less severe parasuicidal acts, fewer inpatient days, much better retention (83% vs. 42%), (4) the reframing of BPD as a treatable diagnosis, (5) the Monday-morning implication: refer for full-model DBT when available; at minimum use skills group and hierarchical targeting. Attending tone. Close with caveats about small all-female sample and the stronger self-harm effect than mood/interpersonal effect.

## 22. Bateman Mentalization-Based Treatment - Bateman & Fonagy 1999

**Citation:** Bateman A, Fonagy P. Effectiveness of partial hospitalization in the treatment of borderline personality disorder: a randomized controlled trial. *Am J Psychiatry.* 1999;156(10):1563-1569.
**File:** `22-Bateman-MBT-1999.m4a`

### The Brief
Bateman and Fonagy's 1999 trial demonstrated that BPD is treatable by a second distinct psychotherapy - Mentalization-Based Treatment - adding empirical weight to Linehan's claim and complicating the "only DBT works for BPD" narrative. They randomized 38 patients with BPD to either (a) an 18-month psychoanalytically-oriented partial hospitalization program centered on MBT (individual therapy, group therapy, expressive therapy, community meetings) or (b) general psychiatric care. At 18 months, the MBT group had significantly fewer self-harm acts, fewer suicide attempts, fewer psychiatric admissions, and less severe depressive symptoms. The gains widened rather than narrowed in the 18-month post-treatment follow-up, and an 8-year naturalistic follow-up (published later) showed the gap persisted. MBT is theoretically distinct from DBT - its target is the capacity to reflect on mental states (one's own and others') in emotionally activated moments - but the outcome profile is similar. **Monday morning:** DBT is not the only evidence-based treatment for BPD. MBT is a legitimate alternative, particularly in settings where analytically-trained clinicians are available. What the two approaches share - long duration, team-based delivery, explicit targeting of emotion dysregulation and interpersonal dysfunction - may matter as much as what separates them. **Caveat:** small sample, single site, and MBT training infrastructure remains thinner than DBT's in most countries.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Bateman & Fonagy 1999 AJP, the first MBT partial-hospitalization RCT for BPD. Structure: (1) the context that Linehan's 1991 trial had put DBT on the map and the question was whether any other approach could match it, (2) the design: N=38, 18-month partial hospitalization built around MBT vs. general psychiatric care, (3) the results: fewer self-harm acts, fewer attempts, fewer admissions, less depression - with gains widening in follow-up, (4) MBT's theoretical target (reflecting on mental states under affective load), (5) the Monday-morning implication: MBT is a legitimate evidence-based alternative to DBT. Attending tone. Close with caveats about small N and MBT's thinner training infrastructure.

## 23. Wampold Common Factors - Wampold 2001

**Citation:** Wampold BE. *The Great Psychotherapy Debate: Models, Methods, and Findings.* Mahwah, NJ: Lawrence Erlbaum; 2001. (Key meta-analytic chapters published concurrently.)
**File:** `23-Wampold-Common-Factors-2001.m4a`

### The Brief
Wampold's book-length synthesis is the rigorous empirical case for the "dodo bird verdict" - the claim that all bona fide psychotherapies produce roughly equivalent outcomes, and that most of the variance in outcome is explained by factors common to all therapies (alliance, empathy, expectancy, allegiance, therapist effects) rather than specific techniques. Wampold marshaled meta-analyses of direct comparisons between bona fide psychotherapies and showed that between-therapy effect sizes are tiny (~0.20) compared to the between-therapist effect sizes (~0.30-0.50) and to the effect of therapy versus no therapy (~0.80). His argument is not that techniques are irrelevant but that they work through, and are dwarfed by, common factors - and that therapist competence and the quality of the alliance are far better predictors of outcome than which branded manual is being used. The claim was controversial in 2001 and remains so, but twenty years of subsequent research has broadly confirmed the pattern for adult psychotherapy of depression and anxiety. **Monday morning:** when a patient isn't improving, don't reflexively reach for a different manualized modality - look first at the alliance, the therapist, and the fit. When you choose a therapist for a patient, the therapist's skill and interpersonal match matter more than the model's brand name. **Caveat:** the dodo bird verdict is stronger for the "neurotic" spectrum (depression, anxiety, adjustment) than for specific conditions where technique-specific advantages exist (exposure for OCD, CBT-I for insomnia, DBT for BPD self-harm).

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Wampold 2001 "The Great Psychotherapy Debate" and the common factors argument. Structure: (1) the specificity-vs.-common-factors debate in psychotherapy research, (2) Wampold's core empirical claim: between-therapy effect sizes are tiny (~0.20) vs. therapist effects (~0.30-0.50) vs. therapy vs. no therapy (~0.80), (3) the implication that alliance, empathy, expectancy, allegiance, and therapist effects dominate specific techniques, (4) the Monday-morning implication: when therapy isn't working, audit the alliance and the therapist before switching modalities, (5) the caveat that specific-technique advantages exist for OCD (exposure), insomnia (CBT-I), BPD (DBT). Attending tone.

## 24. Shedler Psychodynamic Meta-Analysis - Shedler 2010

**Citation:** Shedler J. The efficacy of psychodynamic psychotherapy. *Am Psychol.* 2010;65(2):98-109.
**File:** `24-Shedler-Psychodynamic-2010.m4a`

### The Brief
By 2010 the narrative in mainstream psychiatry was that CBT was the only evidence-based psychotherapy - a claim that rested on publication counts and guideline documents more than on the actual meta-analytic evidence base. Shedler's *American Psychologist* paper is the corrective. Shedler reviewed eight meta-analyses encompassing 160 studies of psychodynamic psychotherapy and short-term dynamic therapy for adult psychopathology. Effect sizes (Cohen's d) were in the 0.7-1.0 range for global symptom improvement - equal to or larger than commonly cited effect sizes for CBT and antidepressant medication. More striking, effect sizes *increased* after the end of treatment in the follow-up period - a pattern that Shedler attributed to the therapy setting in motion ongoing psychological change rather than simply suppressing symptoms while the patient is in treatment. The paper is partly polemical but the meta-analytic numbers are solid. **Monday morning:** when you refer a patient for psychodynamic or psychoanalytically-informed psychotherapy, you are not compromising on evidence - you are choosing an approach with effect sizes comparable to CBT that may produce larger delayed gains. **Caveat:** the trials in Shedler's review are heterogeneous in population, duration, and rigor; the "effect sizes grow after termination" claim is true in some subgroups but not all, and head-to-head trials versus CBT for specific conditions (OCD, PTSD, panic) still favor exposure-based approaches.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Shedler 2010 American Psychologist, "The efficacy of psychodynamic psychotherapy." Structure: (1) the pre-2010 narrative that CBT was the only evidence-based psychotherapy and why Shedler pushed back, (2) the synthesis of eight meta-analyses, 160 studies, (3) the result: Cohen's d of 0.7-1.0, comparable to CBT and antidepressants, (4) the provocative finding that effect sizes increased after termination in follow-up, (5) the Monday-morning implication: referring for psychodynamic therapy is not a compromise on evidence. Attending tone. Close with caveats about heterogeneous trials and specific-condition advantages for exposure-based CBT.

## 25. Norcross Alliance - Norcross 2011

**Citation:** Norcross JC, Lambert MJ. Psychotherapy relationships that work II. *Psychotherapy (Chic).* 2011;48(1):4-8. (Part of the APA Division 29 Task Force report.)
**File:** `25-Norcross-Alliance-2011.m4a`

### The Brief
Norcross and Lambert's APA Division 29 Task Force synthesis is the authoritative summary of the empirical evidence on what *relationship* elements of psychotherapy actually predict outcome, independent of treatment modality. Across dozens of meta-analyses summarized in the 2011 update, the effect of the therapeutic alliance on outcome is robust (r ~ 0.28), exceeding the effect of most specific techniques, and comparably robust for empathy (r ~ 0.30), goal consensus and collaboration (r ~ 0.29), and positive regard. Feedback on client progress, alliance rupture-repair, and client expectations all showed reliable but smaller effects. The task force's language is measured: relationship elements are "demonstrably effective" or "probably effective" rather than being a cure-all. The clinical message is consistent with Wampold's common factors argument but adds granularity about which relational ingredients matter most. **Monday morning:** alliance is not a soft construct - it is one of the largest empirically-documented predictors of psychotherapy outcome. Early alliance checks, explicit goal-setting collaboration, and rupture-repair skills are investments with demonstrable return. If you refer a patient to a therapist and the first three sessions do not produce a sense of alliance, that is clinically actionable information. **Caveat:** alliance-outcome correlations do not resolve the causal direction - early symptom improvement produces alliance as much as alliance produces later improvement.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Norcross & Lambert 2011, the APA Division 29 Task Force update on psychotherapy relationship elements. Structure: (1) the question: independent of modality, which relationship elements actually predict outcome, (2) the meta-analytic findings: alliance r ~ 0.28, empathy r ~ 0.30, goal consensus r ~ 0.29, positive regard and feedback also meaningful, (3) the task force's careful "demonstrably effective" vs. "probably effective" language, (4) the Monday-morning implication: alliance is not soft, it is one of the largest predictors of outcome - early alliance checks and rupture-repair skills matter. Attending tone. Close with the causal-direction caveat.

# ETHICS, DIAGNOSIS, AND TRAUMA

## 26. Rosenhan Pseudopatient - Rosenhan 1973

**Citation:** Rosenhan DL. On being sane in insane places. *Science.* 1973;179(4070):250-258.
**File:** `26-Rosenhan-Pseudopatient-1973.m4a`

### The Brief
Rosenhan's 1973 *Science* paper is the single most cited challenge to the reliability of psychiatric diagnosis in the 20th century. Eight "pseudopatients" - all mentally healthy researchers and volunteers - presented to 12 different psychiatric hospitals claiming to hear a voice saying "empty," "hollow," or "thud." All eight were admitted; all but one were diagnosed with schizophrenia. Once admitted, the pseudopatients behaved normally and reported that their symptoms had resolved, but their lengths of stay averaged 19 days (range 7-52), and they were eventually discharged with diagnoses of "schizophrenia in remission" - never simply "not ill." Normal behavior on the ward was routinely interpreted as pathological by staff (note-taking became "writing behavior" in charts). The paper reframed diagnostic labels as sticky, self-reinforcing, and context-dependent, and arguably accelerated the push toward operationalized criteria that culminated in DSM-III. More recent archival work has raised questions about whether Rosenhan's methodology was as clean as reported, but the paper's conceptual legacy is independent of its empirical specifics. **Monday morning:** when you carry a diagnosis forward in a chart, interrogate it. Ask yourself what observations would falsify it. Be especially skeptical when "normal" behavior on a unit is being reinterpreted as diagnostic confirmation. **Caveat:** Susannah Cahalan's 2019 investigation cast doubt on several of Rosenhan's claimed case details and some pseudopatients may not have existed; the paper should be read as a provocation, not a trial.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Rosenhan 1973 Science, "On being sane in insane places." Structure: (1) the state of psychiatric diagnosis before DSM-III and the reliability problem, (2) the pseudopatient design: 8 sane volunteers, 12 hospitals, "empty" or "hollow" auditory complaint, (3) the findings: all admitted, all but one labeled schizophrenic, average 19-day stay, discharge as "schizophrenia in remission," normal behavior re-read as pathology, (4) the legacy - push toward operationalized DSM-III criteria, (5) the Monday-morning implication: interrogate inherited diagnoses, ask what would falsify them, be skeptical when normal behavior is recoded as sickness. Close with the caveat that subsequent investigation has raised concerns about the paper's methodological honesty.
