## 27. Appelbaum Capacity to Consent - Appelbaum & Grisso 1988

**Citation:** Appelbaum PS, Grisso T. Assessing patients' capacities to consent to treatment. *N Engl J Med.* 1988;319(25):1635-1638.
**File:** `27-Appelbaum-Capacity-1988.m4a`

### The Brief
Appelbaum and Grisso's NEJM essay is the paper every psychiatrist should reread before every capacity consult. They argue that decisional capacity is not a global cognitive trait but a specific, decision-by-decision assessment, and they operationalize it through four now-canonical criteria: (1) **understanding** - the ability to comprehend information about the illness and the treatment, (2) **appreciation** - the ability to apply that information to one's own situation (including insight into illness), (3) **reasoning** - the ability to manipulate information rationally and weigh consequences, and (4) **expressing a choice** - the ability to communicate a stable decision. Each criterion is independently assessable and the level of capacity required can legitimately scale with the stakes of the decision (a sliding scale for high-risk refusals). The paper reframed capacity assessment from an intuitive clinical judgment to a structured, reproducible, defensible process. **Monday morning:** when you are called for a capacity consult, document the four criteria separately. Do not conclude "lacks capacity" globally - specify which criterion is deficient, for which decision, with which evidence. Remember that a patient can have capacity to refuse a low-stakes intervention and simultaneously lack it for a high-stakes one. **Caveat:** these are clinical guidelines, not legal standards - jurisdictions vary, and capacity assessments exist on a spectrum with legal competence determinations that require different evidentiary thresholds.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Appelbaum & Grisso 1988 NEJM, "Assessing patients' capacities to consent to treatment." Structure: (1) the pre-1988 state of capacity assessment as an intuitive clinical judgment, (2) the four criteria: understanding, appreciation, reasoning, expressing a choice, (3) the key insight that capacity is decision-specific, not global, and scales with stakes, (4) the Monday-morning implication: document the four criteria separately for each consult, specify which one is deficient, for which decision. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that these are clinical guidelines, not legal standards, and that jurisdictions vary.

## 28. Bush-Francis Catatonia Rating Scale - Bush 1996

**Citation:** Bush G, Fink M, Petrides G, Dowling F, Francis A. Catatonia. I. Rating scale and standardized examination. *Acta Psychiatr Scand.* 1996;93(2):129-136.
**File:** `28-Bush-Francis-Catatonia-1996.m4a`

### The Brief
Catatonia is the syndrome that psychiatry almost forgot. Dismissed as a subtype of schizophrenia in DSM-III and -IV, catatonia was under-recognized and under-treated for a generation. Bush, Fink, Petrides, Dowling, and Francis changed that with the Bush-Francis Catatonia Rating Scale (BFCRS) - a standardized 23-item examination that operationalizes catatonic signs and gives them inter-rater reliability. The companion paper (Bush 1996, Part II) added a treatment protocol: lorazepam challenge (1-2 mg) with observation for acute response, followed by scheduled lorazepam titration, with ECT for non-responders. The key empirical finding across these papers and subsequent work is that catatonia responds to benzodiazepines or ECT in roughly 70-80% of cases regardless of underlying diagnosis (major depression, bipolar disorder, schizophrenia, or a general medical condition). The BFCRS made the syndrome recognizable and the protocol made it treatable. **Monday morning:** any patient with immobility, mutism, staring, posturing, waxy flexibility, negativism, or stereotypy deserves a BFCRS screen and a lorazepam challenge. Response to 2 mg of lorazepam within 30 minutes is both diagnostic and therapeutic. Missing catatonia is a preventable harm. **Caveat:** the BFCRS is a screening and severity tool, not a diagnostic certainty; malignant catatonia and neuroleptic malignant syndrome overlap significantly and require urgent medical workup in parallel.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Bush, Fink, Petrides, Dowling & Francis 1996, the Bush-Francis Catatonia Rating Scale. Structure: (1) the under-recognition of catatonia after DSM-III subsumed it into schizophrenia, (2) the BFCRS as a 23-item standardized examination that made catatonic signs reliably identifiable, (3) the companion lorazepam challenge protocol and the 70-80% response rate regardless of underlying diagnosis, (4) the Monday-morning implication: screen with BFCRS and do a lorazepam challenge any time you see immobility, mutism, posturing, or waxy flexibility. Attending tone. Close with the caveat about malignant catatonia / NMS overlap requiring parallel medical workup.

## 29. Foa Prolonged Exposure - Foa 2005

**Citation:** Foa EB, Hembree EA, Cahill SP, et al. Randomized trial of prolonged exposure for posttraumatic stress disorder with and without cognitive restructuring: outcome at academic and community clinics. *J Consult Clin Psychol.* 2005;73(5):953-964.
**File:** `29-Foa-Prolonged-Exposure-2005.m4a`

### The Brief
Foa's 2005 JCCP trial established prolonged exposure (PE) as a front-line evidence-based psychotherapy for PTSD and tested whether adding cognitive restructuring improved outcomes. Foa and colleagues randomized 171 women with chronic PTSD (from assault or other trauma) to either (a) PE alone - imaginal exposure to the trauma memory plus in vivo exposure to avoided cues, delivered over 9-12 weekly sessions - or (b) PE plus cognitive restructuring, or (c) a waitlist control. Both active treatments produced large reductions in PTSD symptoms compared to waitlist (between-group effect sizes d ~ 1.5 on the PSS-I). The surprise was that adding cognitive restructuring to PE did not improve outcomes - PE alone was as effective as the combination. The trial also demonstrated that PE could be delivered effectively in community settings by newly trained clinicians, not just at specialized academic centers. **Monday morning:** for chronic PTSD, prolonged exposure is the treatment with the strongest evidence base. Do not dilute it with "supportive therapy" or avoid the exposure component because you fear retraumatization - the evidence says the exposure *is* the active ingredient. Refer to a PE-trained therapist when available. **Caveat:** dropout rates in exposure trials are nontrivial (~20-30%), and patients with severe dissociation or active substance dependence often need stabilization before PE is feasible.

Attending tone. Close with the caveat about dropout rates and the need for stabilization in severely dissociative or substance-dependent patients.

## 30. TADS Adolescent Depression - March 2004

**Citation:** March J, Silva S, Petrycki S, et al (TADS Team). Fluoxetine, cognitive-behavioral therapy, and their combination for adolescents with depression: Treatment for Adolescents With Depression Study (TADS) randomized controlled trial. *JAMA.* 2004;292(7):807-820.
**File:** `30-TADS-Adolescent-MDD-2004.m4a`

### The Brief
TADS is the definitive comparative effectiveness trial for adolescent major depression. The TADS Team randomized 439 adolescents ages 12-17 with moderate-to-severe MDD to one of four conditions: (a) fluoxetine alone, (b) cognitive-behavioral therapy alone, (c) combined fluoxetine plus CBT, or (d) placebo. The 12-week acute outcome, measured by clinical global improvement, was striking. Combined treatment was the clear winner at 71% response, followed by fluoxetine alone at 61%, then CBT alone at 43%, and placebo at 35%. Notably, CBT monotherapy did not separate significantly from placebo at 12 weeks in this trial - a finding that surprised the field and shifted prescribing patterns. Equally important, suicidal events (ideation and attempts) occurred in 15 patients across the study, clustered in the fluoxetine-only arm; combined treatment appeared to have a protective effect on suicidality relative to fluoxetine alone. Later follow-up showed all three active treatments converging around 80% response by 36 weeks. **Monday morning:** for moderate-to-severe adolescent depression, combined fluoxetine plus CBT is the first-line treatment. If CBT is unavailable, fluoxetine alone is acceptable but requires explicit monitoring for suicidality, especially in the first four weeks. **Caveat:** the early CBT null result may reflect the specific CBT manual used and the enrollment of moderate-to-severe cases; later follow-up and different CBT variants show better effects.

# PERSONALITY DISORDERS AND SUICIDE

## 31. Zanarini MSAD 16-Year Follow-up - Zanarini 2012

**Citation:** Zanarini MC, Frankenburg FR, Reich DB, Fitzmaurice G. Attainment and stability of sustained symptomatic remission and recovery among patients with borderline personality disorder and axis II comparison subjects: a 16-year prospective follow-up study. *Am J Psychiatry.* 2012;169(5):476-483.
**File:** `31-Zanarini-MSAD-16yr-2012.m4a`

### The Brief
Zanarini's McLean Study of Adult Development (MSAD) fundamentally rewrote the prognosis of borderline personality disorder. Before MSAD, BPD was widely considered a chronic, disabling, treatment-refractory condition - a diagnosis patients carried for life. Zanarini and colleagues prospectively followed 290 patients hospitalized with rigorously diagnosed BPD and 72 axis II comparison subjects over 16 years, reassessing symptoms at 2-year intervals. The results were startling. By 16 years, 99% of BPD patients had achieved symptomatic remission lasting at least 2 years, 78% achieved sustained remission lasting 8 years, and 60% met full recovery criteria (remission plus good social and vocational functioning). Impulsivity symptoms (self-harm, substance use) remitted earliest and most completely; affective symptoms (chronic emptiness, abandonment fears) were more persistent. Only about 10% of patients who achieved sustained remission relapsed. **Monday morning:** when you are working with a patient with BPD - in the ED, on the unit, in clinic - remember that this is not a life sentence. Communicate hope explicitly; patients and families need to hear that the natural history is toward recovery, especially from the self-harm and impulsive behaviors that dominate the acute presentation. **Caveat:** MSAD patients were all initially hospitalized and received ongoing care; remission rates may be lower in under-treated community populations, and "recovery" in MSAD included functional criteria that many patients still find hard to meet.

Attending tone. Close with the caveat that MSAD patients received ongoing care and community outcomes may differ.

## 32. Gunderson BPD NEJM Review - Gunderson 2011

**Citation:** Gunderson JG. Clinical practice. Borderline personality disorder. *N Engl J Med.* 2011;364(21):2037-2042.
**File:** `32-Gunderson-BPD-NEJM-2011.m4a`

### The Brief
Gunderson's 2011 NEJM clinical practice review is the single best short synthesis of modern BPD for non-psychiatric physicians and for trainees who want the field in 2,500 words. Gunderson, who spent a career defining and studying BPD, distills four things. First, BPD is common (~1.4% general population, ~6% primary care, ~20% inpatient psychiatry) and frequently missed or misdiagnosed as bipolar, depression, or PTSD. Second, the diagnosis rests on interpersonal hypersensitivity - a core trait from which the impulsivity, affective instability, self-harm, and identity disturbance follow. Third, effective treatment exists. Gunderson emphasizes Good Psychiatric Management (GPM) - a structured but non-specialized approach that any competent psychiatrist can deliver - alongside the evidence-based specialist therapies (DBT, MBT, TFP, schema therapy). Fourth, medications have adjunctive roles only; no medication is FDA-approved for BPD and polypharmacy is rampant and harmful. **Monday morning:** make the diagnosis openly and share it with the patient. Prioritize a structured relationship with a consistent clinician over medication escalation. Use GPM principles (clear frame, between-session crisis plan, validation, focus on interpersonal triggers) even if you cannot access formal DBT or MBT. **Caveat:** Gunderson's GPM framework is clinically rich but the evidence base for GPM as superior to other approaches is modest; the strongest RCT evidence still belongs to DBT and MBT.

# SUICIDE AND SAFETY

## 33. Mann Suicide Prevention Strategies - Mann 2005

**Citation:** Mann JJ, Apter A, Bertolote J, et al. Suicide prevention strategies: a systematic review. *JAMA.* 2005;294(16):2064-2074.
**File:** `33-Mann-Suicide-Prevention-2005.m4a`

### The Brief
Mann and colleagues produced the landmark systematic review that organized suicide prevention evidence into a usable framework. Reviewing interventions from 1966 to 2005, they evaluated seven strategies: physician education, public education, gatekeeper training, means restriction, screening, media guidelines, and treatment of psychiatric disorders. Two strategies emerged with the strongest evidence: (1) physician education in depression recognition and treatment, exemplified by the Gotland Study and its replications, which reduced suicide rates by roughly 60% in the intervention communities, and (2) means restriction - reducing access to lethal methods such as firearms, pesticides, analgesics in blister packs, and bridge barriers - which consistently reduced method-specific and often overall suicide rates. Other interventions showed weaker or mixed evidence. Screening alone did not reduce suicide; gatekeeper training had modest effects; media guidelines reduced imitation suicides. **Monday morning:** when you are discharging a patient with suicide risk, the two interventions with the best evidence are getting the depression treated competently and restricting access to lethal means. Every discharge conversation with a family member should include a specific, concrete plan to secure or remove firearms, medications, and other lethal means. Ask about them by name, not in the abstract. **Caveat:** the Mann review predates the modern era of digital interventions and caring-contact studies; the evidence base has expanded, but means restriction remains the most consistent finding across all subsequent reviews.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Mann et al 2005 JAMA, the systematic review of suicide prevention strategies. Structure: (1) the seven strategies Mann evaluated (physician education, public education, gatekeeper training, means restriction, screening, media guidelines, treatment of psychiatric disorders), (2) the two strongest findings: physician education in depression (~60% reduction in Gotland) and means restriction (consistent method-specific and often overall suicide reduction), (3) the weaker findings: screening alone, gatekeeper training, media guidelines, (4) the Monday-morning implication: every discharge for a suicidal patient requires a concrete means-restriction conversation naming firearms, medications, and other lethal means. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that the evidence base has evolved since 2005 but means restriction remains the most consistent finding.

## 34. Stanley Safety Planning Intervention - Stanley & Brown 2012

**Citation:** Stanley B, Brown GK. Safety planning intervention: a brief intervention to mitigate suicide risk. *Cogn Behav Pract.* 2012;19(2):256-264.
**File:** `34-Stanley-Brown-Safety-Planning-2012.m4a`

### The Brief
Stanley and Brown introduced the Safety Planning Intervention (SPI) to replace the discredited "no-suicide contract." A no-suicide contract asks the patient to promise not to kill themselves - a promise with no evidence of effectiveness and real potential for harm (patients conceal ideation to avoid breaking the contract). SPI instead builds a six-step written, collaborative plan with the patient: (1) recognize warning signs, (2) internal coping strategies the patient can use alone, (3) social contacts and settings that provide distraction, (4) people the patient can ask for help, (5) professionals and crisis lines to contact, and (6) a concrete means-restriction plan. The intervention takes 20-45 minutes and is delivered at the point of maximum risk - typically in the ED or at discharge. A 2018 JAMA Psychiatry study by Stanley and colleagues tested SPI plus structured follow-up in VA EDs and found a 45% reduction in suicidal behavior over 6 months compared with usual care. **Monday morning:** every patient leaving your care with suicidal ideation or a recent attempt deserves a completed safety plan you built together - not handed to them as a pamphlet. Use the template, write it by hand or print it, give the patient the original, and keep a copy in the chart. If you are still using no-suicide contracts, stop today. **Caveat:** SPI is not a treatment for the underlying disorder and should never substitute for access to ongoing care; the 45% reduction came with structured follow-up, not from the plan alone.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Stanley & Brown 2012, the Safety Planning Intervention. Structure: (1) why no-suicide contracts were discredited and potentially harmful, (2) the six steps of SPI: warning signs, internal coping, social distraction, people to ask for help, professionals and crisis lines, means restriction, (3) the 2018 JAMA Psychiatry RCT showing a 45% reduction in suicidal behavior with SPI + structured follow-up in VA EDs, (4) the Monday-morning implication: every suicidal patient leaving your care deserves a collaboratively built safety plan, not a contract or a pamphlet. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that SPI is not a treatment for the underlying disorder and the 45% reduction required structured follow-up, not just the plan.

## 35. Franklin Suicide Meta-Analysis - Franklin 2017

**Citation:** Franklin JC, Ribeiro JD, Fox KR, et al. Risk factors for suicidal thoughts and behaviors: a meta-analysis of 50 years of research. *Psychol Bull.* 2017;143(2):187-232.
**File:** `35-Franklin-Suicide-MetaAnalysis-2017.m4a`

### The Brief
Franklin and colleagues conducted the largest meta-analysis ever assembled on suicide risk factors - 365 studies, 3,428 effect sizes, spanning 50 years of research. The result was humbling. No category of risk factor - demographics, psychopathology, prior self-harm, cognitive, social, biological, stressful life events - produced better than weak prediction of suicidal thoughts or behaviors. Predictive accuracy across the literature was near chance, and had not improved in 50 years. Prior suicide attempt, long considered the strongest clinical predictor, produced only a weighted odds ratio around 2.1 for future suicide - real, but far from the near-certainty clinicians sometimes attribute to it. Franklin concluded that traditional static risk factor models are fundamentally limited and that the field needs new approaches: machine learning on large datasets, ecological momentary assessment, and dynamic near-term predictors rather than lifetime categorical factors. **Monday morning:** stop practicing "risk stratification" as if you can reliably sort patients into high-risk and low-risk bins. You cannot. Instead, treat every patient with suicidal ideation as someone who needs access, connection, means restriction, and good treatment of their psychiatric disorder, regardless of whether they score "high" or "low" on a scale. The evidence says the scales are not up to the job. **Caveat:** Franklin's message is about population-level statistical prediction; it does not mean clinical judgment and known warning signs (a suicide plan, access to means, acute intoxication) should be ignored. Those remain actionable even if their positive predictive value is modest.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt

Structure: (1) the scale: 365 studies, 3,428 effect sizes, 50 years, (2) the humbling result - no category of risk factor predicted suicide better than weak, accuracy near chance, no improvement over 50 years, (3) the specific finding that prior attempt produces OR ~ 2.1, not the near-certainty clinicians often assume, (4) Franklin's call for new approaches: machine learning, ecological momentary assessment, dynamic near-term predictors, (5) the Monday-morning implication: stop treating risk stratification as meaningful; every suicidal patient needs access, connection, means restriction, and good treatment. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that actionable acute warning signs (plan, means, intoxication) still matter even if population-level prediction is weak.

# GENETICS AND NEUROSCIENCE

## 36. Caspi 5-HTTLPR  Life Stress - Caspi 2003

**Citation:** Caspi A, Sugden K, Moffitt TE, et al. Influence of life stress on depression: moderation by a polymorphism in the 5-HTT gene. *Science.* 2003;301(5631):386-389.
**File:** `36-Caspi-5HTTLPR-2003.m4a`

### The Brief
Caspi's 2003 Science paper was the most cited and most celebrated gene-environment interaction finding in psychiatry. Using the Dunedin birth cohort (N = 847 followed prospectively from birth through age 26), Caspi and colleagues reported that a functional polymorphism in the serotonin transporter gene promoter (5-HTTLPR) moderated the effect of stressful life events on depression. Individuals carrying one or two copies of the short ("s") allele developed more depressive symptoms, more major depressive episodes, and more suicidality in response to stressful life events than individuals homozygous for the long ("l") allele. The finding was biologically plausible, statistically significant, and immediately attractive - it seemed to open a door to personalized risk prediction and targeted prevention. Over the next 15 years, however, attempted replications produced mixed results, and a 2019 multi-cohort analysis by Border et al (Paper 37) using vastly larger samples definitively failed to replicate the interaction. **Monday morning:** the Caspi paper is a teaching case in how psychiatric genetics is done now, not a clinically actionable finding. Do not order 5-HTTLPR testing. Do not tell patients their serotonin transporter genotype predicts their response to stress or to SSRIs. **Caveat:** the original finding may still be true in some form but requires a sample size and analytic approach the 2003 field did not have; the broader lesson is that candidate-gene  environment findings in psychiatry have largely not replicated.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Caspi et al 2003 Science, the 5-HTTLPR  life stress interaction. Structure: (1) the context of looking for gene-environment interactions in depression, (2) the Dunedin cohort design (N=847 followed birth to age 26) and the headline finding that short allele carriers became more depressed in response to life stress, (3) the initial excitement and why it seemed clinically transformative, (4) the later failures to replicate and the 2019 Border et al definitive non-replication, (5) the Monday-morning implication: this is a teaching case in how psychiatric genetics evolved; do not order 5-HTTLPR testing or counsel patients based on it. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that the broader lesson is about candidate-gene  environment findings in psychiatry failing to replicate.

## 37. Border Candidate Gene Non-Replication - Border 2019

**Citation:** Border R, Johnson EC, Evans LM, et al. No support for historical candidate gene or candidate gene-by-interaction hypotheses for major depression across multiple large samples. *Am J Psychiatry.* 2019;176(5):376-387.
**File:** `37-Border-Candidate-Gene-Null-2019.m4a`

### The Brief
Border and colleagues produced the paper that effectively closed the candidate gene era in psychiatry. Using data from the UK Biobank, 23andMe, and the Psychiatric Genomics Consortium - total samples in the hundreds of thousands, roughly two orders of magnitude larger than the original candidate gene studies - they tested 18 historical candidate genes for depression (including 5-HTTLPR, BDNF, COMT, MAOA, and others) for main effects and for gene  environment interactions with stressful life events and childhood trauma. The result was unambiguous: no candidate gene showed a significant main effect on depression, and no candidate gene  environment interaction replicated. The original findings were consistent with false-positive results driven by small samples, publication bias, and flexible analytic choices. Border's paper aligned psychiatric genetics with modern genome-wide association study (GWAS) approaches that find common psychiatric disorders are highly polygenic - thousands of variants each contributing tiny effects - not a handful of decisive candidate genes. **Monday morning:** do not counsel patients based on candidate gene testing, and be skeptical of commercial "pharmacogenomic" panels that rely on weak candidate gene logic. The field has moved to polygenic risk scores and whole-genome methods. **Caveat:** this does not mean genetics is irrelevant to depression - twin studies still show ~35% heritability - but the signal is distributed across the genome, not concentrated in any one gene you can test.

Structure: (1) the context of the candidate gene era and the 2003 Caspi finding, (2) Border's design: 18 historical candidate genes for depression tested in UK Biobank, 23andMe, and PGC - total samples in the hundreds of thousands, (3) the unambiguous null result: no main effects, no GxE interactions replicated, (4) the implication that the original findings were small-sample false positives and that psychiatric disorders are highly polygenic, (5) the Monday-morning implication: do not counsel patients on candidate gene tests or commercial pharmacogenomic panels built on candidate logic. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that depression is still ~35% heritable - genetics matters, but the signal is distributed across the genome.

## 38. Sekar C4 and Schizophrenia - Sekar 2016

**Citation:** Sekar A, Bialas AR, de Rivera H, et al. Schizophrenia risk from complex variation of complement component 4. *Nature.* 2016;530(7589):177-183.
**File:** `38-Sekar-C4-Schizophrenia-2016.m4a`

### The Brief
Sekar's 2016 Nature paper is a genuine neurobiological breakthrough in schizophrenia - the first time a GWAS signal at a specific locus was dissected down to a causal mechanism. Prior GWAS studies had repeatedly identified the major histocompatibility complex (MHC) region on chromosome 6 as the strongest genetic signal for schizophrenia, but the MHC is gene-dense and the specific causal variant was unknown. Sekar and colleagues, working at the Broad Institute, demonstrated that complex structural variation at the C4 gene, which encodes complement component 4, drives the signal. Higher-expression C4 alleles produce more C4 protein, more C4 is associated with increased risk for schizophrenia (odds ratio modest but real), and C4 functions at the synapse to tag connections for pruning by microglia. This links a genetic risk factor to an established developmental process - synaptic pruning in adolescence - and explains why schizophrenia characteristically emerges in late adolescence and early adulthood when pruning is most active. **Monday morning:** when a patient or family asks "what causes schizophrenia?", you now have a specific neurobiological story to tell that is honest and hopeful - genetic risk acts on a normal developmental process (synaptic pruning) that, when dysregulated, produces the clinical disorder. This is not yet actionable for diagnosis or treatment, but it is the kind of mechanism that drug development requires. **Caveat:** C4 is one factor among thousands contributing to schizophrenia polygenic risk; it is not a diagnostic test and does not explain most cases individually.

Structure: (1) the context that GWAS had consistently identified the MHC region as the strongest schizophrenia signal but not the specific causal gene, (2) Sekar's finding that complex structural variation at C4 drives the MHC signal - higher-expression alleles produce more C4 protein and more schizophrenia risk, (3) the mechanism: C4 tags synapses for pruning by microglia, linking genetic risk to adolescent synaptic pruning, (4) why this explains the characteristic late-adolescent onset of schizophrenia, (5) the Monday-morning implication: you now have an honest, hopeful neurobiological story to offer patients and families - risk acts on a normal developmental process. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that C4 is one factor among thousands contributing to polygenic risk and is not a diagnostic test.

# PERSONALITY AND BOUNDARIES

## 39. Kernberg Borderline Personality Organization - Kernberg 1967

**Citation:** Kernberg O. Borderline personality organization. *J Am Psychoanal Assoc.* 1967;15(3):641-685.
**File:** `39-Kernberg-BPO-1967.m4a`

### The Brief
Kernberg's 1967 paper introduced the concept of Borderline Personality Organization (BPO) - a structural, developmental framework that predated DSM-III and shaped how modern clinicians think about personality pathology at a level deeper than checklist diagnosis. Kernberg argued that between neurotic and psychotic personality organization lies a distinct structural level characterized by three features: (1) identity diffusion - an unstable, fragmented sense of self and of others, (2) primitive defenses - splitting, projective identification, denial, omnipotence, devaluation, and (3) generally intact reality testing that collapses under intense affect or interpersonal stress. BPO is a broader construct than DSM borderline personality disorder; it encompasses severe narcissistic, antisocial, and schizoid presentations as well. Kernberg's structural model became the theoretical foundation for Transference-Focused Psychotherapy (TFP), which uses the therapy relationship as the primary site for integrating split representations of self and other. **Monday morning:** when you see a patient whose presentation changes dramatically from session to session - idealizing you one week, devaluing you the next, with seemingly contradictory accounts of the same events - you are probably seeing splitting and identity diffusion. Kernberg's framework gives you a vocabulary for what you are watching, and it predicts that integration (not merely symptom suppression) is the therapeutic task. **Caveat:** Kernberg's model is theoretically rich but empirically harder to operationalize than the DSM criteria; TFP's RCT evidence base is real but smaller than DBT's, and the framework has largely been absorbed into rather than replaced by modern evidence-based approaches.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Kernberg 1967, Borderline Personality Organization. Structure: (1) the historical context - pre-DSM-III, when personality pathology was understood structurally rather than categorically, (2) Kernberg's three features of BPO: identity diffusion, primitive defenses (especially splitting), intact-but-fragile reality testing, (3) how BPO is broader than DSM BPD and includes severe narcissistic, antisocial, schizoid presentations, (4) the link to Transference-Focused Psychotherapy (TFP) and the therapeutic task of integration, (5) the Monday-morning implication: recognize splitting and identity diffusion clinically and understand that integration is the goal. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that Kernberg's model is theoretically rich but empirically harder to operationalize and that TFP's RCT base is smaller than DBT's.

## 40. Gabbard Boundaries and Boundary Violations - Gabbard 1995

**Citation:** Gabbard GO, Lester EP. *Boundaries and Boundary Violations in Psychoanalysis.* New York: Basic Books; 1995. (Key paper: Gabbard GO. The early history of boundary violations in psychoanalysis. *J Am Psychoanal Assoc.* 1995;43(4):1115-1136.)
**File:** `40-Gabbard-Boundaries-1995.m4a`

### The Brief
Gabbard's work on boundaries and boundary violations - summarized in the 1995 book with Lester and the companion JAPA paper - is the definitive framework for thinking about how the therapeutic relationship can go wrong and how to prevent it. Gabbard distinguishes boundary crossings (small, usually benign deviations from the usual frame - a self-disclosure, a hug at termination, a session that runs long) from boundary violations (progressive, exploitative deviations that harm the patient and typically culminate in sexual contact, business entanglement, or other role reversal). Gabbard's key insight is that boundary violations almost never begin with a single dramatic act. They begin with small boundary crossings that feel justified in context, followed by secrecy, rationalization, and progressive escalation. The classic "slippery slope" goes: self-disclosure -> extended sessions -> sessions outside the office -> meals together -> gifts -> special treatment -> sexual contact. Gabbard's framework informs virtually every modern professional ethics code and training on clinician-patient relationships. **Monday morning:** if you find yourself keeping something about a patient interaction secret from your supervisor, your consultation group, or your spouse, that is the warning sign. Not the interaction itself - the secrecy. Bring it to consultation immediately. Treat boundary crossings as events worth discussing, not events worth hiding. **Caveat:** rigid boundary rules can themselves be clinically counterproductive (e.g., never self-disclosing at all can impair alliance); Gabbard's framework requires clinical judgment, not mechanical application.

Structure: (1) the distinction between boundary crossings (small, usually benign) and boundary violations (progressive, exploitative, harmful), (2) Gabbard's key insight that violations almost never start with a dramatic act - they start with small crossings followed by secrecy and rationalization, (3) the classic slippery slope from self-disclosure through extended sessions to sexual contact or role reversal, (4) the Monday-morning implication: the diagnostic sign is secrecy from your supervisor or consultation group, not the interaction itself; treat any temptation to hide a patient interaction as the moment to bring it to consultation. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that rigid boundary rules can themselves be counterproductive and the framework requires clinical judgment.

# SYSTEMS, SERVICES, AND DIAGNOSIS

## 41. Engel Biopsychosocial Model - Engel 1977

**Citation:** Engel GL. The need for a new medical model: a challenge for biomedicine. *Science.* 1977;196(4286):129-136.
**File:** `41-Engel-Biopsychosocial-1977.m4a`

### The Brief
Engel's 1977 Science paper is the most influential conceptual statement in modern medicine. Writing as an internist and psychiatrist at the University of Rochester, Engel argued that the dominant biomedical model - reducing illness to disordered biology and excluding the patient as a person from the diagnostic and therapeutic process - was scientifically incomplete and clinically inadequate. He proposed the biopsychosocial model: illness and health are products of interacting biological, psychological, and social systems, and competent clinical care requires attention to all three levels simultaneously. The biopsychosocial model provided the conceptual scaffolding for the integration of psychiatry into general medicine, for family medicine as a specialty, for behavioral medicine, and ultimately for the movement toward patient-centered care. Virtually every contemporary psychiatric formulation - a four-axis "bio-psycho-social-spiritual" case write-up, for example - descends from Engel's framework. **Monday morning:** when you are writing a formulation for a patient, force yourself to write something real in each of the biological, psychological, and social sections - not placeholder sentences. If you cannot, you do not yet know the patient. The formulation is the work of integrating what is often given to you as disconnected data, and it is where your clinical judgment actually lives. **Caveat:** the biopsychosocial model has been criticized as so inclusive that it can degenerate into eclectic list-making without a theory of how the levels interact; Engel's original paper is richer and more disciplined than many of its descendants.

Structure: (1) the context of the 1970s biomedical model and its reduction of illness to disordered biology, (2) Engel's argument that competent care requires simultaneous attention to biological, psychological, and social systems, (3) the downstream impact: integration of psychiatry into general medicine, family medicine as a specialty, patient-centered care, (4) the Monday-morning implication: writing a real formulation requires substantive content in each of the three sections, not placeholders - formulation is where clinical judgment lives. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that the biopsychosocial model can degenerate into eclectic list-making without a theory of how the levels interact.

## 42. Insel RDoC - Insel 2010

**Citation:** Insel T, Cuthbert B, Garvey M, et al. Research Domain Criteria (RDoC): toward a new classification framework for research on mental disorders. *Am J Psychiatry.* 2010;167(7):748-751.
**File:** `42-Insel-RDoC-2010.m4a`

### The Brief
Insel's 2010 RDoC paper announced a fundamental shift in how the National Institute of Mental Health would fund research on psychiatric disorders. Insel argued that DSM categories, while useful for clinical communication and billing, had failed as biological research targets - there is no reliable neurobiological signature for "major depressive disorder" or "schizophrenia" because these categories are heterogeneous and overlapping. The Research Domain Criteria (RDoC) framework proposed organizing research around transdiagnostic domains of function - negative valence systems, positive valence systems, cognitive systems, social processes, arousal and regulatory systems, sensorimotor systems - assessed across units of analysis from genes through circuits to behavior. The goal was to let biology and neuroscience, rather than 19th-century nosology, carve psychiatric problems at their natural joints. RDoC is explicitly a research framework, not a clinical diagnostic system; Insel was clear that DSM remained necessary for clinical practice. **Monday morning:** RDoC does not change what you write in a chart tomorrow, but it changes how you should read new psychiatry literature. When a paper reports that a biomarker distinguishes "depressed patients from controls," ask which depression - because the DSM category is heterogeneous - and whether the finding sorts patients along a dimension (e.g., anhedonia severity) that might cross diagnostic boundaries. **Caveat:** fifteen years in, RDoC has generated a sophisticated research vocabulary but has not yet produced the hoped-for biological classifications or transformed clinical practice; the DSM categories, for all their flaws, remain the daily working tools.

Attending tone. Close with the caveat that 15 years in, RDoC has generated research vocabulary but has not yet transformed clinical practice.

## 43. ACT Stein & Santos - Stein & Santos 1998

**Citation:** Stein LI, Santos AB. *Assertive Community Treatment of Persons with Severe Mental Illness.* New York: W. W. Norton; 1998. (Foundational source: Stein LI, Test MA. Alternative to mental hospital treatment. I. Conceptual model, treatment program, and clinical evaluation. *Arch Gen Psychiatry.* 1980;37(4):392-397.)
**File:** `43-Stein-Test-ACT-1980.m4a`

### The Brief
Stein and Test's 1980 Archives paper launched Assertive Community Treatment (ACT), the most thoroughly studied community-based care model for people with severe mental illness. In the Madison (Wisconsin) Program of Assertive Community Treatment (PACT), Stein, Test, and Marx randomized patients with severe psychiatric illness to either (a) usual hospital-based care or (b) a mobile, multidisciplinary team that delivered treatment, case management, crisis intervention, medication management, vocational support, and help with activities of daily living in the community, 24/7. The ACT team took full clinical responsibility for enrolled patients with an explicit low staff-to-patient ratio (roughly 1:10) and shared caseloads. Outcomes were dramatic: hospitalization days dropped by half or more, community tenure increased, quality of life improved, and costs were at least neutral when hospitalization savings were counted. ACT was subsequently replicated dozens of times across the US, UK, and Europe and became a core element of community mental health policy. **Monday morning:** when you are discharging a patient with severe persistent mental illness and multiple prior admissions, ask whether an ACT team exists in your catchment area and whether the patient qualifies. For the right patients - those with high readmission risk, poor medication adherence, and significant functional impairment - ACT is the single community intervention with the strongest evidence for keeping people out of the hospital. **Caveat:** ACT's effect on hospitalization depends on baseline usage - it works best where usual care involves frequent admissions; when implemented in already-low-admission systems, the effect is smaller.

Attending tone. Close with the caveat that ACT's effect depends on baseline hospitalization rates and is smaller in already-low-admission systems.

## 44. ACE Study - Felitti 1998

**Citation:** Felitti VJ, Anda RF, Nordenberg D, et al. Relationship of childhood abuse and household dysfunction to many of the leading causes of death in adults: the Adverse Childhood Experiences (ACE) Study. *Am J Prev Med.* 1998;14(4):245-258.
**File:** `44-Felitti-ACE-Study-1998.m4a`

### The Brief
The Adverse Childhood Experiences (ACE) Study is the foundational empirical link between childhood trauma and adult physical and mental health. Felitti, Anda, and colleagues surveyed 17,337 adult Kaiser Permanente HMO members in San Diego about seven categories of childhood adversity: physical abuse, sexual abuse, emotional abuse, witnessing domestic violence, parental substance use, parental mental illness, and parental incarceration (later expanded to ten). The sample was middle-class, insured, predominantly white - not a high-risk population. Yet exposures were common (more than half had at least one ACE; ~6% had four or more), and the dose-response relationship was striking and linear. An ACE score of 4 or more, compared to 0, produced a 4-12 fold increase in risk for alcoholism, depression, and suicide attempts; a 2-4 fold increase in smoking, poor self-rated health, and STIs; and a 1.4-1.6 fold increase in physical inactivity and severe obesity. Risk for heart disease, cancer, COPD, and liver disease tracked with ACE score. **Monday morning:** ask about ACEs in every initial evaluation, not as a checklist but as a trauma-informed conversation. A patient presenting with medically unexplained symptoms, refractory depression, or substance use may be telling you, through their symptoms, about a developmental history you have not asked about. **Caveat:** the ACE score is an epidemiological dose metric, not a clinical tool; a high score does not determine destiny, and resilience factors (stable adult relationships, access to care) modify outcomes substantially.

# ADDICTION

## 45. Project MATCH - Project MATCH Research Group 1997

**Citation:** Project MATCH Research Group. Matching alcoholism treatments to client heterogeneity: Project MATCH posttreatment drinking outcomes. *J Stud Alcohol.* 1997;58(1):7-29.
**File:** `45-Project-MATCH-1997.m4a`

### The Brief
Project MATCH was the largest psychotherapy trial ever conducted for alcohol use disorder and one of the largest psychotherapy trials in any condition. The NIAAA-funded study randomized 1,726 adults with alcohol dependence or abuse to one of three manualized 12-week psychotherapies: (a) Cognitive-Behavioral Therapy (CBT), (b) Twelve-Step Facilitation (TSF), or (c) Motivational Enhancement Therapy (MET, a 4-session condensed motivational interviewing approach). The design was a matching study - the hypothesis was that certain patient characteristics (anger, psychiatric severity, social network support for drinking, gender, meaning-seeking) would predict which treatment a given patient would respond to best. The matching hypothesis largely failed. On most matching variables, patients did similarly well across all three treatments. The more important finding was the absolute effect: all three active treatments produced large, sustained reductions in drinking - roughly 60% reduction in drinking days and a doubling of abstinent days - maintained at 1-year and 3-year follow-up. **Monday morning:** for alcohol use disorder, deliver whichever evidence-based psychotherapy is available and that you can deliver competently - CBT, TSF, or MET. Do not agonize about matching patients to a specific modality; the active ingredient is structured, competent, evidence-based care of any of these three flavors. **Caveat:** Project MATCH enrolled motivated treatment-seekers in specialty alcohol settings; the findings should not be assumed to apply to unmotivated or severely comorbid patients in general psychiatric settings without modification.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Project MATCH 1997, the alcohol use disorder psychotherapy matching trial. Structure: (1) the design: N=1,726, three manualized 12-week psychotherapies (CBT, Twelve-Step Facilitation, Motivational Enhancement Therapy), matching hypothesis based on patient characteristics, (2) the headline finding that the matching hypothesis largely failed - patients did similarly well across all three treatments, (3) the equally important finding of large sustained reductions in drinking (roughly 60% fewer drinking days, maintained at 3 years), (4) the Monday-morning implication: deliver whichever of the three you can competently - matching is less important than getting structured evidence-based care. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that Project MATCH enrolled motivated treatment-seekers and findings may not generalize to unmotivated or severely comorbid patients.

---

## 46. COMBINE - Anton 2006

**Citation:** Anton RF, O'Malley SS, Ciraulo DA, et al (COMBINE Study Research Group). Combined pharmacotherapies and behavioral interventions for alcohol dependence: the COMBINE study: a randomized controlled trial. *JAMA.* 2006;295(17):2003-2017.
**File:** `46-Anton-COMBINE-2006.m4a`

### The Brief
COMBINE was the definitive comparative effectiveness trial for pharmacotherapy in alcohol dependence. Anton and colleagues randomized 1,383 recently alcohol-dependent adults to a factorial combination of (a) naltrexone 100 mg/day or placebo, (b) acamprosate 3 g/day or placebo, and (c) with or without Combined Behavioral Intervention (CBI), a structured alcohol-focused psychotherapy, delivered over 16 weeks. All patients received Medical Management - a structured 20-minute check-in with a clinician to monitor adherence, side effects, and drinking. The headline results were two-fold. First, naltrexone plus medical management was comparably effective to naltrexone plus CBI, meaning that a competent primary care clinician delivering structured medical management can get results as good as specialty behavioral therapy - a finding with massive implications for access to treatment. Second, acamprosate showed no benefit in this US sample, either alone or combined - a surprising result given robust European evidence for acamprosate and one that remains partially unexplained (possibly reflecting different enrollment criteria, shorter abstinence before randomization, and concurrent medications). **Monday morning:** if you are a psychiatrist, internist, or primary care physician, you can treat alcohol use disorder with naltrexone plus structured medical management and expect results comparable to specialty therapy. Naltrexone is FDA-approved, oral or injectable, and dramatically underprescribed. **Caveat:** COMBINE's acamprosate null result is not definitive - European trials still support it - but clinicians should not reflexively choose acamprosate as first-line over naltrexone based on theory alone.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Anton et al 2006 JAMA COMBINE, the alcohol dependence pharmacotherapy trial. Structure: (1) the factorial design: N=1,383, naltrexone vs. placebo, acamprosate vs. placebo, with or without Combined Behavioral Intervention, all over medical management, 16 weeks, (2) the two headline findings: naltrexone + medical management was comparable to naltrexone + specialty CBI, and acamprosate showed no benefit in the US sample, (3) the access implication: a primary care physician with structured medical management can deliver effective alcohol use disorder treatment, (4) the Monday-morning implication: prescribe naltrexone for alcohol use disorder; medical management suffices even without specialty therapy. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that the acamprosate null is not definitive and European evidence still supports it.

---

## 47. Volkow Addiction Brain Disease - Volkow 2016

**Citation:** Volkow ND, Koob GF, McLellan AT. Neurobiologic advances from the brain disease model of addiction. *N Engl J Med.* 2016;374(4):363-371.
**File:** `47-Volkow-Addiction-Brain-2016.m4a`

### The Brief
Volkow, Koob, and McLellan's 2016 NEJM review is the most authoritative synthesis of the brain disease model of addiction. The paper lays out three neurobiological systems that together explain the clinical phenomenology of addiction: (1) the reward system - mesolimbic dopamine pathway - which is hijacked by drugs that produce abnormally large dopamine releases and then downregulates, producing tolerance and anhedonia; (2) the stress and antireward system - extended amygdala, corticotropin-releasing factor, dynorphin - which becomes sensitized during withdrawal and drives the dysphoria and craving that precipitate relapse; and (3) the executive control system - prefrontal cortex - which is weakened, reducing the ability to inhibit drug-seeking even when the patient consciously wants to stop. The three-system model explains why addiction is characterized by compulsive use despite harm, craving triggered by stress and cues, and impaired executive control - all observable clinically and all with converging imaging and animal model evidence. Volkow argues this model has direct clinical and policy implications: addiction is a chronic relapsing brain disease, not a moral failing, and should be treated medically with evidence-based medications (buprenorphine, methadone, naltrexone, varenicline) as a core element of care. **Monday morning:** when a patient with opioid use disorder relapses, do not treat it as a moral failure - it is the predictable consequence of a neurobiological process. Offer buprenorphine or methadone; these are the interventions with the strongest evidence for reducing overdose death. **Caveat:** the brain disease model has been criticized for potentially minimizing the social and motivational dimensions of addiction; the strongest defenses of the model, including Volkow's own writing, explicitly treat it as complementary to psychosocial frameworks, not a replacement.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Volkow, Koob & McLellan 2016 NEJM, the brain disease model of addiction. Structure: (1) the three neurobiological systems: reward (mesolimbic dopamine hijack and downregulation), stress/antireward (extended amygdala sensitization driving relapse), executive control (prefrontal weakening of inhibition), (2) how each system maps to a clinical phenomenon: tolerance and anhedonia, craving and relapse, loss of control, (3) the policy and clinical implication: addiction is a chronic relapsing brain disease, not a moral failing, and should be treated medically, (4) the Monday-morning implication: when a patient with OUD relapses, do not treat it as a moral failure; offer buprenorphine or methadone, which have the strongest evidence for reducing overdose death. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that the brain disease model complements rather than replaces psychosocial frameworks.

---

# CHILD, ADOLESCENT, AND GERIATRIC

## 48. MTA Study - MTA Cooperative Group 1999

**Citation:** MTA Cooperative Group. A 14-month randomized clinical trial of treatment strategies for attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder. The Multimodal Treatment Study of Children with ADHD. *Arch Gen Psychiatry.* 1999;56(12):1073-1086.
**File:** `48-MTA-Study-1999.m4a`

### The Brief
The Multimodal Treatment of ADHD (MTA) study was the largest and longest RCT ever conducted in child psychiatry. The MTA Cooperative Group randomized 579 children ages 7-9 with DSM-IV ADHD combined type to one of four 14-month treatment arms: (a) optimized medication management (typically methylphenidate, carefully titrated and monitored monthly), (b) intensive behavioral therapy (parent training, school consultation, summer treatment program), (c) combined medication + behavioral therapy, or (d) community care (the usual treatment children received in their communities, which often included medication but less intensively managed). Outcomes at 14 months were clear and informative. Optimized medication management and combined treatment were both significantly better than intensive behavioral therapy alone or community care for core ADHD symptoms. Combined treatment was modestly superior to medication alone, particularly for comorbid symptoms (anxiety, ODD, academic, and family functioning) and allowed use of lower medication doses. Community care produced worse outcomes despite similar medication use, suggesting that the way medication is managed - dose optimization, frequent monitoring - matters as much as the choice of medication. **Monday morning:** for ADHD combined type, stimulant medication is the single most effective element of treatment, but how you manage it matters: titrate carefully, monitor monthly, and add behavioral intervention for comorbid symptoms and family functioning. Do not assume that any stimulant prescription is equivalent to MTA-style treatment. **Caveat:** long-term follow-up (36 months and later) showed convergence of treatment groups and loss of the medication advantage, suggesting that adherence, naturalistic dose adjustment, and developmental factors matter - the 14-month advantage is not permanent without ongoing optimization.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on the MTA Study 1999, multimodal ADHD treatment. Structure: (1) the design: N=579 children ages 7-9 with ADHD combined type randomized to optimized medication management, intensive behavioral therapy, combined, or community care over 14 months, (2) the results: optimized meds and combined were superior to intensive behavioral or community care; combined was modestly better than meds alone for comorbid symptoms and functioning, (3) the access message that community care underperformed despite similar medication use - management matters, (4) the Monday-morning implication: for ADHD combined type, carefully-managed stimulants are first-line; add behavioral intervention for comorbid symptoms. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that long-term follow-up showed convergence, so sustained benefit requires ongoing optimization.

---

## 49. Bridge SSRI-Suicidality Meta-Analysis - Bridge 2007

**Citation:** Bridge JA, Iyengar S, Salary CB, et al. Clinical response and risk for reported suicidal ideation and suicide attempts in pediatric antidepressant treatment: a meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials. *JAMA.* 2007;297(15):1683-1696.
**File:** `49-Bridge-SSRI-Youth-2007.m4a`

### The Brief
Bridge and colleagues produced the most careful quantitative synthesis of antidepressant trials in children and adolescents after the 2004 FDA black box warning. The meta-analysis included 27 placebo-controlled RCTs of SSRIs and SNRIs in youth for major depressive disorder, obsessive-compulsive disorder, and non-OCD anxiety disorders (total N > 5,000). Two findings defined the paper. First, antidepressants were clearly effective: the pooled NNT to produce one responder was 10 for MDD, 6 for OCD, and 3 for non-OCD anxiety. Second, the risk of suicidal ideation or attempts was small but real: the pooled risk difference was 0.7% (1% on active medication vs. 0.3% on placebo) across all conditions - the NNH was approximately 143. Crucially, there were no completed suicides in any trial. The MDD signal for suicidality was strongest; the anxiety and OCD signals were weaker. The clinical implication was clear: the benefit-to-harm ratio was favorable across all three conditions, with NNT consistently better than NNH, and the benefit was largest in anxiety and OCD where the suicidality signal was smallest. **Monday morning:** the 2004 FDA black box warning is about a real but small risk. Do not withhold SSRIs from children and adolescents who need them. Do have an explicit conversation with patients and families about the signal, document the conversation, and monitor closely - especially in the first four weeks. Fluoxetine has the strongest MDD evidence base and is FDA-approved down to age 8. **Caveat:** the suicidality signal is for ideation and attempts, not completed suicide, and likely reflects activation, akathisia, or disinhibition in a small subset of youth - monitoring is the mitigation.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Bridge et al 2007 JAMA, the pediatric antidepressant meta-analysis. Structure: (1) the context of the 2004 FDA black box warning on SSRIs in youth, (2) the design: 27 placebo-controlled RCTs in pediatric MDD, OCD, and non-OCD anxiety, total N > 5,000, (3) the headline efficacy numbers: NNT 10 for MDD, 6 for OCD, 3 for anxiety, (4) the suicidality signal: pooled risk difference 0.7%, NNH ~143, no completed suicides, strongest signal in MDD, (5) the favorable benefit-harm ratio with NNT better than NNH across all conditions, (6) the Monday-morning implication: do not withhold SSRIs from children who need them; have the explicit black-box conversation and monitor closely. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that the signal is ideation and attempts not completed suicide, and monitoring is the mitigation.

---

## 50. Reynolds Geriatric Depression Maintenance - Reynolds 2006

**Citation:** Reynolds CF III, Dew MA, Pollock BG, et al. Maintenance treatment of major depression in old age. *N Engl J Med.* 2006;354(11):1130-1138.
**File:** `50-Reynolds-Geriatric-Maintenance-2006.m4a`

### The Brief
Reynolds's 2006 NEJM trial is the definitive study of maintenance treatment for depression in late life. Reynolds and colleagues randomized 116 patients age 70 and older who had responded to acute treatment of major depression with paroxetine plus interpersonal psychotherapy (IPT) to one of four two-year maintenance conditions: (a) paroxetine plus monthly IPT, (b) paroxetine plus monthly clinical management, (c) placebo plus monthly IPT, or (d) placebo plus monthly clinical management. The results were decisive on the medication question and more nuanced on the psychotherapy question. Paroxetine prevented recurrence dramatically compared to placebo: the recurrence rate was 35% on paroxetine versus 68% on placebo, with NNT of about 3 - one of the strongest maintenance effects in the entire depression literature. Monthly IPT alone (with placebo) did not significantly prevent recurrence. The combination of paroxetine plus monthly IPT was not superior to paroxetine plus clinical management. The implication was that in geriatric depression, antidepressant medication continuation is the single most important element of relapse prevention, and monthly psychotherapy is not a substitute. **Monday morning:** when an older adult achieves remission from major depression, continue the antidepressant that got them well. Do not discontinue prematurely because of age or polypharmacy concerns; the recurrence rate off medication is prohibitively high. Duration of maintenance in first-episode geriatric MDD should be at least 1-2 years and often longer, with shared-decision discussion about risks and benefits. **Caveat:** Reynolds enrolled patients who had already responded to acute paroxetine + IPT, so the findings speak to recurrence prevention, not to initial treatment selection; also, paroxetine has substantial anticholinergic burden and falls risk in older adults, and sertraline or escitalopram are typically preferred first-line in modern practice.

### NotebookLM Customize Prompt
Create a Brief Audio Overview (hard target: 3 minutes runtime; do not exceed 4 minutes total; prioritize concision over completeness) on Reynolds et al 2006 NEJM, maintenance treatment of geriatric depression. Structure: (1) the design: N=116 patients age 70+ who had responded to acute paroxetine + IPT, randomized to 2-year maintenance with paroxetine + monthly IPT, paroxetine + clinical management, placebo + monthly IPT, or placebo + clinical management, (2) the decisive result: 35% recurrence on paroxetine vs. 68% on placebo, NNT ~3, (3) the nuance: monthly IPT alone did not prevent recurrence and added nothing to paroxetine + clinical management, (4) the Monday-morning implication: when an older adult remits from MDD, continue the antidepressant; do not discontinue prematurely, and plan for at least 1-2 years of maintenance. Attending tone. Close with the caveat that Reynolds's findings apply to recurrence prevention not acute selection, and paroxetine itself has anticholinergic and falls risks so modern practice often prefers sertraline or escitalopram.

---

*End of briefs. 50 of 50 complete.*

# Incorporation Plan - OpenEvidence "Landmark Psychiatry" into the MS3 Clerkship Library

*Drafted from `OPENEVIDENCE REVIEWS.docx` (parsed 2026-06-28). Faculty planning doc - not student-facing.*

## 1. What the document actually is

Three OpenEvidence outputs in one file:

| Block | Content | Count | Where it goes |
|---|---|---|---|
| A. The 50 Landmark Articles | Per-paper: why-it-matters, key findings, clinical pearls,  importance, difficulty, "still practice-changing?", follow-up | 50 papers (deduped from 2 partial copies) | New **Landmark Trials** evidence section + cross-links |
| B. Final Synthesis | Top-20, **6-week progressive reading order**, high-yield table, the canon | - | Wires into Week 1-6 pages |
| C. Practice-Changing 2020-Present | FDA approvals, RCTs, meta-analyses, systems of care | 16 items | New **"What's New (2020-present)"** page |

**Audience = student/clinician (evidence layer), not patients.** This belongs in the clerkship hub's `07_Evidence_and_Reading`, *not* the patient `psychoed-library/`. Naming it "psychoeducation" in conversation is fine, but routing matters: these are trial summaries for MS3s, so they live with the curriculum, behind the same faculty-attestation gate as the rest of the student site.

## 2. The backbone is already built (Phase 0 - done)

Parsed into a single source of truth that all three workstreams (your pages, Codex quizzes, NotebookLM audio) key off:

- `LM_master_index.csv` / `.json` - 50 papers, IDs **LM-01...LM-50**, with topic bucket,  importance, difficulty, DOI, verbatim citation (+ PubMed IDs).
- `reading_order_by_week.md` - the 6-week order + Top-20.
- `practice_changing_2020plus.csv` - the 16 recent items (PC-01...PC-16).

**Quality check:** 49/50 carry a DOI (the lone gap, #47 Delay & Deniker 1952, is genuinely pre-DOI). I verified a sample against Crossref - CATIE `10.1056/NEJMoa051688` -> exact match (Lieberman, NEJM 2005;353:1209). DOIs were taken verbatim from each paper's own citation line, not fuzzy-matched.

**Topic distribution of the 50:** Psychosis 9 * Mood 9 * Acute & Safety 8 * Skills/Therapy/Systems 8 * Geriatric/Delirium 7 * Substance Use 4 * Anxiety/Trauma/OCD 4 * Personality 1. *(Perinatal and Psychopharmacology drew 0 - that material is in the 2020+ block; see 7 gap note.)*

## 3. Shared IDs + naming convention (so the 3 workstreams auto-link)

The single most important coordination decision: **everything keys off the LM ID.**

| Workstream | Owner | Unit | Filename convention | Renders as |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Evidence pages | Claude | 50 cards + 6 week blocks + 2020 page | `content/landmark_trials.md` (+ anchors `#lm-01`) | hub pages |
| Quizzes | Codex | per-paper item set | `quiz_LM-01.json` | "Quiz yourself" button on each card |
| Audio overviews | You (NotebookLM) | per-paper (Top 20) + per-week (rest) | `audio_LM-01.mp3` / `audio_week3.mp3` | "Listen" button on each card / week header |

Each card checks for a matching `quiz_*`/`audio_*` asset and lights up the button when present - so you and Codex can drop files in at any time and they appear automatically. **Hand Codex the LM IDs + the quiz JSON schema below; tell NotebookLM to name exports by LM ID or week number.**

```
quiz_LM-01.json  ->  { "id":"LM-01", "items":[ {"stem":"...","options":["..."],"answer":1,"rationale":"...","difficulty":"intermediate","doi":"10.1056/NEJMoa051688"} ] }
```

## 4. Per-paper card template (reusable, renders from the manifest)

```
### {number}. {short_title}   {importance}/5 * {difficulty}
**Bottom line.** {one-sentence takeaway}
**Why it matters / key findings.** {why_matters -> key_findings, paraphrased}
**Clinical pearls.** {bulleted pearls}
**Changes practice?** {practice_changing}
**Source.** {primary_citation}  *  [DOI](https://doi.org/{doi}) * [PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/{pmid})
**On the unit ->** {linked Core-Topic page} * {linked tool}   |    Listen * [yes] Quiz yourself
```

## 5. Wiring into the existing hub (Phase 2)

- **Week pages (1-6):** append " This week's landmark papers" using the doc's 6-week order - which already lines up with your week themes:

  | Hub week | Theme | Landmark papers (LM #) |
  |---|---|---|
  | 1 Foundations | model, alliance, recovery, safety basics | 48, 49, 47, 33, 43, 11, 10, 37 |
  | 2 Mood/Psychosis/Pharm | depression, ECT, ketamine, bipolar | 3,4,36,32,42,20,16,21,13,22,9,14,8,46 |
  | 3 Psychotherapy/Personality | CATIE/clozapine, antipsychotic MA, FEP | 1,2,30,19,17,31,18 |
  | 4 Family/Systems | delirium, catatonia, neurocognitive | 5,6,35,12,39,40,38,50 |
  | 5 Acute/Emergency | DBT, CBT, PTSD, SUD, MI | 7,26,27,28,29,25,23,24,34 |
  | 6 Integration | collaborative care, recovery, systems | 15,41,43,44,45 |

  *(The doc's week labels differ slightly from your hub's; I've aligned them above - confirm or tweak.)*
- **Core Topic pages:** add a "Landmark evidence" callout linking the topic's LM cards (mapping already in the manifest's `topic_bucket`).
- **Tools  papers:** C-SSRS tool  LM-11 (Posner) * Catatonia page  LM-12 (Bush-Francis) * Delirium  LM-05/06 (CAM/HELP) * Withdrawal  COMBINE/buprenorphine (LM-23/24) * Suicide tools  LM-09/10/37.
- **Search:** the existing build auto-indexes new pages - no extra work. I'll add a few synonyms (e.g., `catie`->antipsychotic, `cssrs`->suicide, `help`->delirium) so trial acronyms resolve.

## 6. QA / risk gates (before any student release)

1. **DOI verification - all 49.** Spot-check passed (CATIE [yes]). Run the full set through Crossref; flag any DOI whose title = the cited paper. *(I can do this now.)*
2. **Verify the post-cutoff 2020+ items.** A few reference 2025-2026 events - e.g., "Auvelity agitation-in-Alzheimer's indication (2026)," "psilocybin meta-analysis (Kishi 2026)," "DBT vs SSRI in BPD (AJP 2025)." These are after my knowledge cutoff and are exactly where an AI source can hallucinate. **Flag: verify each against a primary source before publishing.** *(I can web-verify these.)*
3. **Copyright.** OpenEvidence prose is AI-generated; don't paste it verbatim onto the public site. Cards **paraphrase** into your own pearls and **attribute to the original trial/journal**, not "per OpenEvidence."
4. **Editorial judgments are the source's, not yours.** The  importance / difficulty / "still practice-changing?" calls are OpenEvidence's opinions - present as editorial and confirm on attestation.
5. **Attestation gate.** These are evidence claims -> same per-page sign-off as the rest of the hub; ships behind the "pending faculty review" banner until you stamp it.

## 7. Gaps & decisions

- **Perinatal & Psychopharmacology have 0 core papers.** Both are covered in the 2020+ block (zuranolone, brexanolone, Pillinger metabolic MA). Decide whether to promote 1-2 into the core set so every Core-Topic page has a landmark anchor. *Recommend: yes - add brexanolone/zuranolone (perinatal) and Leucht/Huhn (already #19/#30) as the psychopharm anchors.*
- **Audio granularity (the one high-leverage choice).** Per-paper audio = 50 NotebookLM runs; per-week = 6. **Recommended default: per-week audio for all six weeks + per-paper audio only for the Top-20.** Naming convention above supports both. Tell me if you'd rather do all-50.

## 8. Phased rollout

- **Phase 0 - done.** Parse -> manifest + DOI spot-check.
- **Phase 1 - verify.** All 49 DOIs + the 2025-26 items; you attest the editorial ratings.
- **Phase 2 - build.** Generate the 50 cards + master index + "What's New 2020-present" page from the manifest; wire week/topic/tool cross-links; add search synonyms.
- **Phase 3 - converge.** Codex quizzes + your NotebookLM audio drop in by LM ID; buttons light up.
- **Phase 4 - ship.** Rebuild search, deploy behind attestation banner, you stamp.

## 9. What I can do next (your call)

a. **Verify** all 49 DOIs + the handful of 2025-26 "practice-changing" claims, and report any that don't check out.
b. **Build Phase 2** now - the Landmark Trials index page + the 2020-present page from the manifest, deployed behind the review banner, with quiz/audio buttons stubbed and ready.
c. **Spec for Codex** - a one-pager giving the LM IDs + quiz JSON schema so its quizzes target the right anchors, plus the audio naming convention for your NotebookLM exports.

*Joshua Moss, MD | Psychiatrist * Educational; evidence claims pending faculty attestation. No PHI.*

# Landmark Psychiatry - Listen & Test

> 50 landmark papers as ~2-minute audio overviews (NotebookLM), grouped by theme. Where a self-test deck exists, use Quiz this paper; where a DOI is verified, open the paper. The Active Recall self-test bank covers these papers plus additional high-yield topics. Suggested, not required. Educational; verify against primary sources.
